SECTION 11 — THE MAGNETRON HYPOTHESIS: MATHEMATICAL FORMULATION, MODEL DEVELOPMENT, AND COSMOLOGICAL FALSIFIABILITY

Research Program: Nexus Sovereign
Human Research Author: Alpha One
AI-Assisted Research and Editorial Contributions: Axiom and other contributing AI systems, subject to documented contribution records
Document Version: 1.1 — Revised Research Draft
Classification: Interdisciplinary Scientific Research

---

11.1. Introduction: From Conceptual Hypothesis to Mathematical Cosmology

The Magnetron Hypothesis is the central cosmological proposition investigated within the Nexus Cosmological Framework. It concerns the possibility that the observable universe may be understood as part of a larger physical arrangement involving cosmological geometry, thermodynamic relationships, electromagnetic organization, and interactions between physically distinguishable domains.

The hypothesis originated from a conceptual analogy with resonant electromagnetic systems, particularly the conventional magnetron. A magnetron is an engineered microwave oscillator in which electrons interact with electromagnetic fields within a resonant structure under applied electric and magnetic fields. Its operation involves identifiable physical components, governing equations, energy inputs, and measurable outputs.

That analogy provides a possible starting point for questions about resonance, bounded systems, and energy transfer. It does not establish that the universe is itself a magnetron, that a cosmological cavity exists, or that energy is transferred into our observable universe from an external physical domain.

Those propositions require independent mathematical and observational justification.

The purpose of this section is to establish a disciplined mathematical development program. It identifies the minimum requirements for converting the Magnetron Hypothesis from a conceptual cosmological proposal into a physically specified, mathematically closed, and observationally testable model.

Four primary questions organize the investigation:

1. Can the observable universe be described within a finite spatial, causal, or otherwise mathematically bounded domain?
2. Can that domain be related to a larger physical system through a consistent mathematical construction?
3. Can a proposed additional physical sector or inter-domain interaction produce measurable cosmological consequences?
4. Do those consequences generate predictions that are distinguishable from established cosmological models?

These questions must remain logically separate.

A finite observable region does not establish a material wall. A compact spatial topology does not establish an external energy source. An additional stress-energy component does not establish an encompassing domain. A resonant mathematical solution does not establish a cosmological magnetron mechanism.

The scientific status of the hypothesis therefore depends on a sequence of increasingly demanding requirements:

- Clear definitions of the physical domains.
- A specified spacetime geometry.
- Defined physical degrees of freedom.
- Mathematically consistent governing equations.
- Appropriate initial and boundary conditions.
- Conservation and stability analysis.
- Quantitative solutions or reproducible numerical predictions.
- Observational consequences distinguishable from established models.
- Independent examination and reproducibility.

This section distinguishes three categories throughout:

Established physical theory: Results already derived within general relativity, relativistic cosmology, electromagnetic theory, or established statistical methodology.

General mathematical modeling frameworks: Equations and structures that can be used to investigate possible extensions without asserting that the proposed physical entities exist.

Nexus-specific propositions: Hypotheses concerning an additional cosmological sector, a magnetron-like mechanism, or interaction with an encompassing domain. These remain unverified until their physical definitions and predictions have been established.

The governing principle is that mathematical compatibility with established theory is necessary but insufficient. A proposed extension must also provide a physical interpretation and a test that can distinguish it from existing explanations.

11.2. Established Cosmological Foundation

11.2.1. Einstein Field Equations

The mathematical starting point is general relativity.

The Einstein field equations relate spacetime curvature to the distribution of energy and momentum:

[
G_{\mu\nu}+\Lambda g_{\mu\nu}

\frac{8\pi G}{c^4}T_{\mu\nu}.
]

Here:

- (G_{\mu\nu}) is the Einstein tensor.
- (\Lambda) is the cosmological constant.
- (g_{\mu\nu}) is the spacetime metric.
- (G) is Newton's gravitational constant.
- (c) is the speed of light.
- (T_{\mu\nu}) is the stress-energy tensor.

The Einstein tensor is constructed from the Ricci curvature tensor and scalar curvature:

[
G_{\mu\nu}

R_{\mu\nu}-\frac12 Rg_{\mu\nu}.
]

The field equations are not a Nexus-specific result. They provide the established relativistic foundation against which any proposed modification or extension must be evaluated.

The contracted Bianchi identities imply:

[
\nabla_\mu G^{\mu\nu}=0.
]

Consequently, when the cosmological constant is constant and the Einstein equations hold,

[
\nabla_\mu T^{\mu\nu}=0.
]

This is the covariant local conservation condition for the total stress-energy tensor in the standard formulation.

It is important to distinguish this relationship from a claim that every expanding cosmological spacetime possesses a unique globally conserved total energy. Local covariant conservation is not equivalent to a simple global energy ledger covering the entire history of an expanding universe.

11.2.2. Friedmann-Lemaître-Robertson-Walker Geometry

The standard large-scale cosmological model commonly begins with a homogeneous and isotropic spacetime described by the Friedmann-Lemaître-Robertson-Walker metric.

With metric signature ((-+++)), the line element is:

[
ds^2

-c^2dt^2
+
a^2(t)
\left[
\frac{dr^2}{1-kr^2}
+
r^2d\Omega^2
\right],
]

where:

[
d\Omega^2=d\theta^2+\sin^2\theta,d\phi^2.
]

The symbols represent:

- (a(t)): cosmological scale factor.
- (t): cosmological proper time.
- (r,\theta,\phi): spatial coordinates.
- (k): normalized spatial-curvature parameter, conventionally (-1), (0), or (+1).

The precise coordinate normalization determines the dimensions assigned to (a(t)) and (r). Physical results must remain invariant under a consistent change of coordinates.

The Hubble parameter is:

[
H(t)=\frac{\dot a(t)}{a(t)}.
]

It describes the fractional expansion rate of the cosmological scale factor.

Using natural units in which (c=1), and expressing (\rho) as energy density, the first Friedmann equation is:

[
H^2

\frac{8\pi G}{3}\rho

\frac{k}{a^2}
+
\frac{\Lambda}{3}.
]

The corresponding acceleration equation is:

[
\frac{\ddot a}{a}

-\frac{4\pi G}{3}(\rho+3p)
+
\frac{\Lambda}{3}.
]

Here (p) is pressure expressed in the same natural-unit convention as the energy density (\rho).

In SI conventions, factors of (c) must be restored consistently. The pressure contribution in the acceleration equation is then expressed through (p/c^2) when (\rho) is represented as mass-equivalent density.

These equations provide the reference description of homogeneous and isotropic cosmological evolution.

They are not a derivation of the Magnetron Hypothesis.

A Nexus extension must demonstrate exactly which assumptions of the reference model it retains, which assumptions it changes, and what new physical content justifies the change.

11.2.3. Reference Model and Scientific Comparison

The base spatially flat six-parameter (\Lambda)CDM model provides a principal observational reference.

It incorporates:

- General relativistic background evolution.
- Cold dark matter.
- Baryonic matter.
- Radiation.
- A cosmological constant.
- Primordial scalar perturbations described by a specified statistical spectrum.

The Planck 2018 cosmological-parameter analysis provides a major reference for this model. Under its stated assumptions and data combinations, it reports approximately:

[
H_0=67.4\pm0.5
\ \mathrm{km,s^{-1},Mpc^{-1}},
]

and:

[
\Omega_m=0.315\pm0.007.
]

These are model-dependent parameter inferences, not universal constants independent of cosmological assumptions.

The reference model is not beyond revision. It is, however, supported by a substantial body of observational and theoretical work. Any proposed extension must account for its established successes and identify the particular observations or physical questions that motivate an alternative description.

A new model should not be evaluated only by whether it can reproduce one selected observation. Its performance must be examined across the relevant observational evidence as a whole.

11.3. Defining the Cosmological Domain

The Magnetron Hypothesis requires precise definitions of the domain under investigation.

Three concepts must be distinguished.

11.3.1. Observable Domain

The observable universe is the region from which information has been able to reach an observer through causal propagation.

Its limits depend on:

- The spacetime geometry.
- The expansion history.
- The speed and paths of signal propagation.
- The age and causal history of the universe.
- The observer's location and worldline.

An observational horizon is not automatically a material surface, physical wall, or enclosing structure.

The cosmic microwave background is relic radiation associated with the epoch of photon decoupling. Its observed last-scattering surface represents a limit to the electromagnetic information directly received from earlier cosmic history. It is not evidence of a material boundary surrounding the universe.

The distinction is fundamental: a limit on what can be observed is not automatically a limit on what physically exists.

11.3.2. Complete Spatial Domain

The complete spatial domain refers to the spatial manifold used to describe the universe at a specified cosmological time.

Its local geometry may be:

- Spatially flat.
- Positively curved.
- Negatively curved.

Its global topology may be simply connected or multiply connected.

A spatial manifold may possess finite volume without possessing a physical boundary. For example, a compact flat three-dimensional torus is a mathematically legitimate spatial construction that has finite volume and no material edge.

Therefore:

[
\text{Finite spatial volume}
\not\Rightarrow
\text{Physical enclosure}.
]

Spatial curvature, topology, volume, and the existence of a physical interface are separate questions.

11.3.3. Hypothetical Encompassing Domain

The Magnetron Hypothesis additionally considers whether the modeled cosmological domain might be related to a larger physical arrangement.

For conceptual purposes, let:

[
\mathcal C
]

denote the cosmological domain under investigation, and let:

[
\mathcal E
]

denote a hypothetical encompassing domain.

The provisional notation:

[
\mathcal C\subseteq\mathcal E
]

may be useful for communicating the concept, but it is not a physical derivation.

Ordinary set inclusion does not establish that two objects belong to compatible spacetime manifolds, possess a shared causal structure, or interact through a physically meaningful interface.

A complete construction would need to specify:

1. The geometry of each domain.
2. Their respective spacetime manifolds.
3. Their causal structures.
4. Their physical fields and degrees of freedom.
5. The mathematical relationship between the domains.
6. Any proposed embedding, mapping, or interface.
7. The governing equations and applicable initial or boundary conditions.

If the two domains are described using different manifolds, the theory must provide a well-defined mathematical mapping or other appropriate construction connecting them.

Until such a construction is derived, (\mathcal E) remains a hypothetical modeling concept.

11.4. Three Distinct Mathematical Development Pathways

The Nexus proposal can be investigated through three mathematically distinct pathways.

They must remain separate unless a later physical derivation establishes a consistent relationship among them.

11.4.1. Model A — Finite or Multiply Connected Cosmological Space

The first pathway investigates whether the universe possesses finite spatial volume or nontrivial global topology.

This possibility does not require an external energy source, a physical wall, or a magnetron-like mechanism.

A spatially flat universe, for example, may possess compact topology. Local spatial curvature can remain zero even though the complete spatial manifold has finite volume.

A three-dimensional torus provides one illustrative mathematical example.

The relevant research question is whether a particular topology produces observational signatures distinguishable from those of a simply connected spatial model.

Potential investigations include:

- Searches for matched patterns in cosmic microwave background maps.
- Correlations between large-scale temperature fluctuations.
- Repeated images of astronomical sources.
- Statistical signatures in harmonic coefficients.
- Constraints on characteristic topological scales.
- Tests using temperature and polarization information.

Matched-circle searches provide one important observational method. The absence of a detected matched-circle signal constrains particular configurations and scales. It does not eliminate every possible topology.

Some spatial configurations can possess short non-contractible loops that do not pass through the observer's location. More general statistical signatures may therefore be required.

This pathway concerns global spatial geometry and topology. Even if a finite topology were established, that result would not independently establish an external physical domain or energy exchange.

11.4.2. Model B — An Additional Cosmological Physical Sector

The second pathway investigates whether the conventional cosmological stress-energy description requires an additional physical component.

Let the proposed additional sector be denoted by (X).

A general decomposition is:

[
T_{\mathrm{total}}^{\mu\nu}

T_{\mathrm{standard}}^{\mu\nu}
+
T_X^{\mu\nu}.
]

The Einstein equations then become:

[
G^{\mu\nu}+\Lambda g^{\mu\nu}

\frac{8\pi G}{c^4}
\left(
T_{\mathrm{standard}}^{\mu\nu}
+
T_X^{\mu\nu}
\right).
]

In this convention, the cosmological constant remains on the geometric side of the equation. It must not also be counted as part of either stress-energy contribution unless the entire formulation is consistently rewritten.

The expression above is a general representation of an additional source contribution.

It does not establish that (X) exists, that it originates outside the observable universe, or that it has a magnetron-like physical interpretation.

The proposed sector must have defined:

- Physical degrees of freedom.
- A stress-energy description.
- Governing equations.
- Constitutive relationships.
- Coupling terms, if interactions are proposed.
- Initial conditions.
- Observable consequences.

Without those elements, (T_X^{\mu\nu}) is a mathematical placeholder rather than a completed physical model.

11.4.3. Model C — Interaction with an Encompassing Physical Domain

The third pathway is the strongest and most speculative interpretation.

It proposes that the cosmological domain may interact with a physically defined encompassing system.

Such an interaction cannot be established by inserting an unexplained source term into the cosmological equations.

A valid model must identify:

1. The physical fields or degrees of freedom involved.
2. The geometry of the interacting domains.
3. The mathematical relationship between those domains.
4. The mechanism of coupling.
5. The relevant interface or interaction conditions.
6. The associated energy-momentum relationships.
7. The governing field or matter equations.
8. The initial and boundary conditions.
9. The measurable consequences.

This pathway requires substantially more development than the topology or additional-sector pathways.

A successful model of finite topology would not establish an external physical sector. A successful additional-sector model would not establish a larger encompassing domain. A successful mathematical interface construction would not establish a magnetron mechanism unless the relevant physical coupling were also derived.

The three pathways must therefore be evaluated independently.

11.5. Preliminary Mathematical Representation of an Additional Sector

For an initial homogeneous and isotropic model, a hypothetical additional sector (X) may be represented by an effective energy density and pressure:

[
\rho_X(t),\qquad p_X(t).
]

The total energy density is then:

[
\rho_{\mathrm{total}}

\rho_{\mathrm{standard}}+\rho_X.
]

Under the FLRW assumptions and in natural units, the Friedmann equation becomes:

[
H^2

\frac{8\pi G}{3}
\left(
\rho_{\mathrm{standard}}+\rho_X
\right)

\frac{k}{a^2}
+
\frac{\Lambda}{3}.
]

This is a standard mathematical form for an additional homogeneous and isotropic source contribution.

It does not identify the source as a magnetron or establish its physical origin.

11.5.1. Equation of State

A simple phenomenological representation is:

[
p_X=w_X\rho_X,
]

where (w_X) is an equation-of-state parameter.

If (X) is separately conserved and (w_X) is constant, its homogeneous continuity equation is:

[
\dot\rho_X+3H(\rho_X+p_X)=0.
]

Substituting the equation of state gives:

[
\dot\rho_X+3H(1+w_X)\rho_X=0.
]

The resulting solution is:

[
\rho_X(a)

\rho_{X,0}a^{-3(1+w_X)},
]

where (a=1) defines the reference normalization and (\rho_{X,0}) is the corresponding energy density.

This scaling relationship is conditional on separate conservation and a constant equation-of-state parameter.

For example, pressureless matter has (w_X=0), while radiation has (w_X=1/3). A cosmological-constant-like component has (w_X=-1) under the usual homogeneous description.

These examples illustrate established mathematical behavior. They do not identify the Nexus additional sector.

If the proposed component exchanges energy with another sector, the continuity equation must include the corresponding interaction term. The separately conserved scaling law can no longer be assumed without justification.

11.5.2. Requirements for a Predictive Sector

A predictive model must specify whether (w_X) is:

- A constant parameter.
- A function of the scale factor.
- A function of a physical field.
- An effective quantity derived from a more fundamental theory.

The model must also identify the permitted parameter range, physical interpretation, and observational constraints.

A value of (w_X) must not be selected solely because it produces a desirable fit.

If the parameter is phenomenological, it must be described as such. If it is derived from a field theory, the derivation must be supplied.

A model that reproduces a reference expansion history through arbitrary parameter selection may have limited explanatory value. A stronger model must demonstrate why its behavior follows from a coherent physical mechanism.

11.6. Energy-Momentum Exchange and Covariant Conservation

A central requirement of the Magnetron Hypothesis is a mathematically consistent treatment of energy and momentum exchange.

For multiple interacting components, one may write:

[
\nabla_\mu T_{(i)}^{\mu\nu}=Q_i^\nu,
]

where:

- (T_{(i)}^{\mu\nu}) is the stress-energy tensor of component (i).
- (Q_i^\nu) represents energy-momentum transfer associated with that component.
- (\nabla_\mu) is the covariant derivative.

For a closed collection of interacting components:

[
\sum_i Q_i^\nu=0.
]

Therefore:

[
\nabla_\mu T_{\mathrm{total}}^{\mu\nu}=0.
]

This is consistent with the covariant conservation requirement associated with the Einstein field equations.

The interaction terms describe transfers between components. They do not create an exception to total covariant conservation.

11.6.1. Homogeneous Energy-Transfer Model

For an illustrative homogeneous cosmological model, consider pressure-bearing matter and the proposed sector (X).

A phenomenological representation is:

[
\dot\rho_m+3H(\rho_m+p_m)=Q,
]

[
\dot\rho_X+3H(\rho_X+p_X)=-Q.
]

Here (Q) is an energy-transfer rate per unit physical volume under the chosen cosmological convention.

With this sign convention:

- (Q>0) transfers energy into the matter sector.
- (Q<0) transfers energy out of the matter sector.
- The corresponding transfer for (X) has the opposite sign.

Adding the two equations gives:

[
\dot\rho_{\mathrm{total}}
+
3H
\left(
\rho_{\mathrm{total}}+p_{\mathrm{total}}
\right)
=0.
]

This background relationship is consistent with the total continuity equation for the combined system.

It is a possible interaction framework, not a derivation of a Nexus-specific magnetron mechanism.

11.6.2. The Physical Origin of the Interaction Term

A complete model must explain the origin of (Q).

It cannot simply assign an arbitrary function to the energy-transfer rate because that function improves agreement with a selected dataset.

The theory must specify:

- Which fields or components interact.
- How the interaction arises.
- The mathematical form of the coupling.
- The dimensions and physical interpretation of its parameters.
- The circumstances under which the interaction becomes significant.
- The consequences for perturbations and structure formation.

The background scalar (Q) represents only one part of the interaction description.

The full covariant exchange vector (Q^\nu) also contains information about momentum transfer. A background energy-transfer equation does not, by itself, provide a complete treatment of spatial perturbations, momentum exchange, or anisotropic effects.

Those elements must be derived before the model can be regarded as a complete cosmological theory.

11.6.3. Local Conservation and Global Energy

A further distinction is essential.

General relativity provides local covariant conservation of the total stress-energy tensor when the Einstein equations and their assumptions apply.

However, an expanding cosmological spacetime does not necessarily possess a unique globally conserved total energy in the simple sense familiar from isolated systems in Newtonian mechanics.

Global conserved quantities generally require appropriate spacetime symmetries or additional geometric structure.

Consequently, the Nexus framework must distinguish:

1. Local covariant conservation.
2. Energy-momentum transfer between specified components.
3. Energy accounting within a defined physical control volume.
4. Global energy statements justified by the relevant spacetime symmetries.

These concepts cannot be substituted for one another.

A proposed external energy source would require a defined physical model and a consistent description of how its energy-momentum enters the cosmological system.

11.7. Boundary Conditions and the Meaning of a Cosmological Cavity

The term cavity is used within Nexus as a conceptual description of a bounded domain.

In electromagnetic engineering, a cavity has a defined physical geometry and boundary conditions. Its fields satisfy equations subject to those conditions.

A cosmological domain does not automatically possess an equivalent material boundary.

Three different mathematical situations must be distinguished.

11.7.1. Cosmological Model Without a Physical Boundary

The spatial universe may be extended without a physical edge.

Its observable region remains limited because of causal propagation and the finite history of observable information.

In this situation, the cosmic microwave background defines an important observational surface associated with the last-scattering epoch. It is not a material wall.

A cosmological model can therefore have an observationally bounded region without requiring an enclosing physical structure.

11.7.2. Compact Spatial Topology Without a Boundary

A universe may have finite spatial volume through its topology without possessing a physical boundary.

A compact spatial manifold can be boundaryless.

This is a legitimate subject of mathematical cosmology and must not be confused with an enclosing shell or material surface.

11.7.3. Physical Interface Between Spacetime Domains

If the Magnetron Hypothesis requires an actual physical interface between two regions, the theory must define its geometry and physical properties.

General relativity provides a mathematical framework for matching spacetime geometries across idealized hypersurfaces. The Israel junction conditions are a well-known formulation for thin-shell constructions.

A specific interface model requires:

- Two specified spacetime regions.
- A matching hypersurface.
- An induced metric on that hypersurface.
- The extrinsic curvature on each side.
- A surface stress-energy tensor, where required.
- Appropriate junction conditions.
- Consistent conservation relationships.

The induced metric describes the geometry intrinsic to the hypersurface. The extrinsic curvature describes how that hypersurface is embedded in the surrounding spacetime.

The junction conditions relate discontinuities in the extrinsic curvature to surface stress-energy under the applicable assumptions.

These conditions provide a mathematical tool. They do not demonstrate that a cosmological interface exists.

No Nexus-specific physical interface has yet been derived.

The CMB must not be substituted for such an interface without an independent physical argument.

11.7.4. Requirements for a Nexus Interface Model

A proposed interface must identify:

1. Its dimensionality and spacetime character.
2. Its geometric embedding or matching construction.
3. The physical fields defined on either side.
4. The continuity or discontinuity conditions.
5. Any surface stress-energy.
6. The energy-momentum exchange relationships.
7. The stability and causal properties.
8. The observable consequences.

If the interface is introduced only as a mathematical boundary condition, it must not be described as an observed physical surface.

If it is proposed as a physical object, its physical properties and observational consequences must be derived.

11.8. The Magnetron Analogy and the Requirements for a Physical Mechanism

A conventional magnetron uses crossed electric and magnetic fields and a resonant structure to generate microwave oscillations from supplied electrical energy.

Its operation involves identifiable physical processes, including electron motion, field interaction, phase organization, and conversion of supplied electrical energy into electromagnetic output.

It does not generate energy from nothing.

The magnetron analogy can inspire questions about resonant systems, field interactions, and energy transfer. It cannot establish that a comparable cosmological mechanism exists.

For the Magnetron Hypothesis to become a physical theory, it must identify an actual mathematical mechanism.

At minimum, this requires:

1. Defined physical degrees of freedom.
2. A field or matter description.
3. Governing equations.
4. Initial and boundary conditions.
5. A coupling mechanism.
6. Energy-momentum accounting.
7. A mathematical solution or reproducible numerical prediction.
8. At least one observable signature.
9. Stability and causal-consistency analysis.
10. A comparison with established physical explanations.

11.8.1. An Illustrative Field-Theory Development Route

One possible theoretical route would introduce an additional covariant field, denoted by (\chi).

An illustrative action may be written:

[
S=
\int d^4x,\sqrt{-g}
\left[
\frac{R-2\Lambda}{16\pi G}
+
\mathcal L_{\mathrm{standard}}
+
\mathcal L_\chi
+
\mathcal L_{\mathrm{interaction}}
\right].
]

Here:

- (S) is the action.
- (g) is the determinant of the spacetime metric.
- (R) is the Ricci scalar.
- (\Lambda) is the cosmological constant.
- (\mathcal L_{\mathrm{standard}}) represents established matter and field contributions.
- (\mathcal L_\chi) describes the proposed additional field.
- (\mathcal L_{\mathrm{interaction}}) describes its proposed coupling to other fields.

This is a general action-based modeling template.

It is not a Nexus-derived action, and it does not yet specify the physical properties of (\chi).

The use of an additional field is one possible modeling route. It is not a requirement that the Magnetron Hypothesis must ultimately be represented by a scalar field. Other possibilities could involve vector fields, tensor fields, matter sectors, modified geometric structures, or a different mathematically consistent formulation.

11.8.2. Requirements for Completing the Action

A complete theory must specify:

- The field's physical interpretation.
- Its kinetic structure.
- Its potential or constitutive relationships.
- The interaction terms.
- The dimensions and allowed ranges of the parameters.
- The symmetries of the action.
- The permitted field configurations.
- The relevant initial conditions.

The governing equations must then be derived by varying the action with respect to the metric and the independent fields.

The field variation gives the field equation for (\chi). Metric variation determines the associated stress-energy contribution and its coupling to spacetime geometry.

The resulting model must be examined for:

1. Mathematical consistency.
2. Dimensional consistency.
3. Appropriate treatment of energy conditions.
4. Stability of background solutions.
5. Stability of perturbations.
6. Causal propagation.
7. Well-posed initial-value behavior.
8. Compatibility with established gravitational observations.

A model that produces a formal solution but contains unstable modes, pathological propagation, or an ill-defined initial-value problem cannot be considered physically complete.

11.8.3. What Would Establish a Magnetron-Specific Mechanism?

The existence of an additional field would not, by itself, establish a magnetron-like cosmological mechanism.

A magnetron-specific interpretation would require a demonstrated relationship between the proposed degrees of freedom and the physical characteristics that motivate the analogy.

The model would need to identify:

- The resonant or oscillatory degrees of freedom.
- The physical meaning of the relevant frequencies or modes.
- The coupling responsible for the proposed interaction.
- The mechanism that regulates or transfers energy.
- The conditions under which the process operates.
- The observable consequences that distinguish it from conventional field dynamics.

If the resulting equations reduce to an already established physical theory without introducing a new prediction, the appropriate conclusion would be that the Nexus construction is an application or interpretation of existing theory rather than a new physical mechanism.

That possibility must remain open.

11.9. Rotation, Anisotropy, and Cosmological Geometry

The original Nexus concept included a rotating-tray analogy in which rotation was proposed as a possible stabilizing feature of a bounded cosmological system.

This concept requires a precise relativistic treatment.

The FLRW model assumes large-scale spatial homogeneity and isotropy. A globally rotating or anisotropically expanding cosmological model generally requires a more general spacetime description.

Rotation must not be inferred from local astrophysical behavior.

Galaxies, accretion disks, stars, and other systems rotate. Their local angular momentum does not establish global rotation of the universe.

11.9.1. Relativistic Vorticity

A relevant quantity is the vorticity tensor associated with a specified timelike fluid congruence.

Using natural units, metric signature ((-+++)), and a normalized four-velocity:

[
u^\mu u_\mu=-1,
]

the spatial projection tensor is:

[
h_{\mu\nu}=g_{\mu\nu}+u_\mu u_\nu.
]

The vorticity tensor is:

[
\omega_{\mu\nu}

h_\mu{}^\alpha h_\nu{}^\beta
\nabla_{[\beta}u_{\alpha]},
]

where antisymmetrization is defined by:

[
\nabla_{[\beta}u_{\alpha]}

\frac12
\left(
\nabla_\beta u_\alpha

\nabla_\alpha u_\beta
\right).
]

The corresponding vorticity magnitude is:

[
\omega

\sqrt{
\frac12\omega_{\mu\nu}\omega^{\mu\nu}
}.
]

This quantity characterizes the rotation of the specified timelike congruence under the chosen geometric and sign conventions.

Its physical interpretation depends on the selected four-velocity field and cosmological model.

11.9.2. Rotation and Anisotropic Expansion Are Not Identical

Vorticity and shear describe different aspects of a congruence.

Vorticity characterizes local rotational behavior. Shear characterizes distortion in the relative shape of neighboring worldlines.

A cosmological model may possess anisotropic expansion without possessing the same rotational properties as another model.

Bianchi cosmologies provide a family of homogeneous but generally anisotropic solutions to Einstein's equations. Not every Bianchi model is rotating.

The Nexus framework must therefore avoid treating global rotation, anisotropic expansion, shear, and vorticity as interchangeable descriptions.

11.9.3. Observational Constraints

A cosmological rotation hypothesis must be tested through the complete observational predictions of its chosen spacetime model.

Relevant observations include:

- CMB temperature anisotropies.
- CMB polarization.
- Large-scale structure.
- Distance-redshift relationships.
- Anisotropic expansion constraints.
- Other observations sensitive to the chosen geometry.

Published analyses of Bianchi cosmologies have placed strong constraints on particular forms of anisotropic expansion using Planck observations. These constraints are model-dependent and must not be interpreted as a universal numerical limit on every conceivable rotating cosmology.

The appropriate requirement is that any proposed Nexus rotation model specify its geometry and calculate the observables that follow from it.

Rotation remains a distinct theoretical possibility, not an assumed property of the Nexus cosmological domain.

11.10. Observational Constraints and Model Compatibility

The Magnetron Hypothesis must be evaluated against established observational evidence.

The relevant datasets depend on the particular model being tested, but may include:

- Cosmic microwave background temperature measurements.
- CMB polarization and lensing measurements.
- Type Ia supernova distance measurements.
- Baryon acoustic oscillation observations.
- Large-scale galaxy clustering.
- Weak gravitational lensing.
- Primordial element abundance measurements.
- Measurements of the expansion rate.
- Other observations relevant to the proposed physical sector or interaction.

The Planck 2018 cosmological-parameter analysis provides an important reference for the base-(\Lambda)CDM model.

Its inferred late-time parameters include approximately:

[
H_0=67.4\pm0.5
\ \mathrm{km,s^{-1},Mpc^{-1}},
]

[
\Omega_m=0.315\pm0.007.
]

These estimates are conditional on the specified cosmological model and data combination.

They must not be treated as model-independent measurements that automatically apply unchanged to every extended cosmological theory.

Any Nexus extension must reproduce the successful predictions of the reference framework within the relevant observational uncertainties or demonstrate a statistically and physically defensible improvement.

A model that improves agreement with one dataset while substantially worsening agreement with another cannot be regarded as successful without a defensible explanation.

11.10.1. Cosmic Topology

Nontrivial cosmic topology remains a legitimate research question.

The Einstein field equations constrain local spacetime curvature but do not, by themselves, uniquely determine global spatial topology.

Searches for matched circles in CMB maps provide one method for constraining certain possible topologies.

A 2023 analysis of orientable Euclidean manifolds reported no generic matched-circle pairs above noise in WMAP or Planck temperature data. Its resulting constraint applies to specified topological configurations and the shortest non-contractible loop through our location. The analysis also emphasizes that, in many configurations, shorter loops may avoid the observer.

The result therefore constrains a defined family of models rather than excluding all possible nontrivial topologies.

Other statistical approaches may be required, depending on:

- The proposed topology.
- The observer's location.
- The sky coverage.
- Foreground treatment.
- The assumed cosmological perturbation model.
- The observational sensitivity.

The finite-domain question remains open for particular mathematical models.

However, the existence of a possible finite topology does not establish an external magnetron mechanism.

11.10.2. Cosmological Rotation and Anisotropy

Analyses of anisotropic cosmological models, including Bianchi-type models, provide important observational constraints.

A physically motivated global-rotation or anisotropic-expansion model must be evaluated through its complete relationship to the background geometry, perturbations, and observational parameters.

An arbitrary anisotropic correction fitted independently to a dataset is insufficient.

The proposed model must demonstrate that its parameters correspond to a physically consistent geometry and produce predictions that can be compared with observations.

11.10.3. Additional Energy Components

An additional cosmological sector must be constrained against the observations relevant to its physical effects.

These may include:

- The expansion history.
- CMB temperature and polarization spectra.
- Structure formation.
- Gravitational lensing.
- Distance measurements.
- The growth of cosmological perturbations.
- Constraints on the matter and radiation content.

The background expansion history alone may not distinguish two models with similar effective energy densities.

A complete analysis may therefore require perturbation equations and predictions for structure formation and CMB observables.

The model must also identify whether its additional sector behaves as a homogeneous fluid, a dynamical field, an interacting component, or another specified physical structure.

11.11. Quantitative Predictions and the Requirement of Distinguishability

The Magnetron Hypothesis must eventually produce at least one quantitative prediction that differs from an appropriate reference model.

Let:

[
M_0
]

denote the selected reference cosmological model, and:

[
M_N
]

denote a specified Nexus extension.

For an observable (O), define:

[
\Delta O=O(M_N)-O(M_0).
]

A useful prediction must be:

- Mathematically defined.
- Physically interpretable.
- Quantitatively calculable.
- Observable in principle.
- Distinguishable under realistic measurement uncertainty.
- Robust against reasonable changes in model assumptions.
- Evaluated using appropriate nuisance-parameter and systematic-error treatments.

A predicted difference smaller than available observational sensitivity may be mathematically interesting but empirically inaccessible.

A difference that can be entirely absorbed by adjustments to existing model parameters may not provide a distinctive empirical test.

The research program must therefore identify observables and parameter combinations that genuinely distinguish the proposed models.

Potential targets include:

- The expansion history (H(z)).
- Distance-redshift relationships.
- CMB angular power spectra.
- Matter power spectra.
- Large-scale anisotropy.
- Spatial curvature constraints.
- Gravitational lensing observables.
- Signatures associated with additional fields or interactions.

11.11.1. Covariance-Weighted Prediction Separation

For a vector of predicted observables, define:

[
\Delta\mathbf O

\mathbf O(M_N)-\mathbf O(M_0).
]

If both prediction vectors are evaluated using an appropriate common observational covariance matrix (C), a preliminary squared prediction-separation measure is:

[
D^2

\Delta\mathbf O^T
C^{-1}
\Delta\mathbf O.
]

This dimensionless quantity describes the covariance-weighted separation between two prediction vectors under the specified assumptions.

It is not, by itself:

- A likelihood ratio.
- A measured improvement in fit.
- A complete model-comparison result.
- Proof that the models can be distinguished after parameter refitting.

The covariance model must be appropriate to the observable vector and its units. The matrix must be invertible or treated using a justified alternative when degeneracies or singular modes are present.

A full analysis must account for:

- Parameter refitting.
- Nuisance parameters.
- Covariance uncertainty.
- Calibration and systematic effects.
- Correlations among observational datasets.
- Model-dependent prediction uncertainty.
- The possibility of overlapping observable distributions.

The calculation of (D^2) is a preliminary diagnostic, not a substitute for a complete likelihood-based analysis.

11.11.2. Predictive Specificity

A Nexus-specific prediction must identify the physical reason for the expected difference.

It is not enough to state that the model may change the expansion history or alter the CMB.

The prediction must specify:

1. The observable.
2. The model parameters controlling it.
3. The expected direction and magnitude of the effect.
4. The reference model.
5. The relevant observational dataset.
6. The expected uncertainty.
7. The conditions under which the prediction would be falsified.

The prediction should be calculated before the corresponding observational result is used to tune the model, wherever feasible.

This reduces the risk of interpreting an accidental fit as evidence for a new physical mechanism.

At the present research stage, no distinctive Nexus-specific cosmological prediction has been established by the mathematical framework presented in this chapter.

11.12. Statistical Model Comparison

A proposed cosmological extension must be compared with existing explanations using appropriate statistical methods.

The likelihood of a dataset (D) under a model (M) is:

[
\mathcal L(D\mid M).
]

Parameter estimation alone is insufficient.

A more complicated model can often fit a dataset more closely simply because it contains additional adjustable parameters.

Model comparison must therefore consider complexity, parameter uncertainty, observational covariance, and the assumptions underlying the chosen statistical method.

Possible tools include:

- Bayesian evidence.
- Information criteria.
- Posterior predictive checks.
- Cross-validation where appropriate.
- Sensitivity analysis.
- Robustness tests under alternative data treatments.
- Parameter-identifiability analysis.

11.12.1. Bayesian Evidence

The Bayesian evidence for a model is:

[
Z_M

P(D\mid M)

\int
P(D\mid\boldsymbol\theta,M)
P(\boldsymbol\theta\mid M)
,d\boldsymbol\theta,
]

where:

- (D) is the dataset.
- (\boldsymbol\theta) is the model parameter vector.
- (P(D\mid\boldsymbol\theta,M)) is the likelihood.
- (P(\boldsymbol\theta\mid M)) is the prior distribution.

The evidence averages the likelihood over the prior parameter space.

A comparison between two models can be expressed through the Bayes factor:

[
B_{10}=\frac{Z_{M_1}}{Z_{M_0}}.
]

The interpretation of a Bayes factor depends on the prior distributions and model definitions.

A model with additional parameters is not automatically favored simply because it can achieve a high maximum likelihood.

The priors must be physically and statistically defensible. Sensitivity to prior choices must be reported.

11.12.2. Bayesian Information Criterion

A commonly used approximate model-selection measure is the Bayesian information criterion:

[
\mathrm{BIC}

k\ln(n)-2\ln(\mathcal L_{\max}),
]

where:

- (k) is the number of fitted parameters.
- (n) is the sample-size quantity appropriate to the statistical approximation.
- (\mathcal L_{\max}) is the maximum likelihood.

Lower BIC values are preferred under the criterion's assumptions.

The BIC is an asymptotic approximation. It is not interchangeable with Bayesian evidence and is not universally appropriate for every cosmological likelihood.

For correlated cosmological datasets, (n) must be interpreted consistently with the likelihood and its assumptions. It must not automatically be equated with the number of entries in a data vector.

The criterion should be used only where its assumptions are sufficiently justified. Other model-comparison methods may be more appropriate for complex likelihoods, strong correlations, or models with unusual parameter structures.

11.12.3. Gaussian Likelihood and Covariance

For a Gaussian likelihood with a fixed covariance matrix (C), a common chi-squared expression is:

[
\chi^2

(\mathbf d-\mathbf m)^T
C^{-1}
(\mathbf d-\mathbf m),
]

where:

- (\mathbf d) is the observed data vector.
- (\mathbf m) is the model prediction.
- (C) is the covariance matrix.

If the covariance matrix depends on model parameters, the determinant contribution must also be included in the Gaussian log-likelihood:

[
-2\ln\mathcal L

\chi^2+\ln|C|+N\ln(2\pi),
]

where (N) is the dimension of the data vector under the stated Gaussian formulation.

The treatment of correlations, nuisance parameters, calibration uncertainties, covariance dependence, and dataset overlap must be documented.

A credible Nexus extension should demonstrate that any improvement in explanatory performance justifies its additional complexity and remains robust under appropriate statistical checks.

11.13. Falsifiability of the Magnetron Hypothesis

A central requirement of this research program is that the hypothesis identify outcomes that would count against it.

The following conditions establish a proposed falsification and rejection framework for specific model versions.

Failure Condition 1 — No Mathematical Closure

If the proposed cosmological mechanism cannot be expressed through a consistent set of governing equations and defined physical variables, it cannot be advanced as a predictive physical model.

Failure Condition 2 — Conservation Inconsistency

If the model violates the required covariant conservation relationships without a mathematically justified extension of the underlying theory, it must be rejected or revised.

Failure Condition 3 — No Distinctive Prediction

If the Nexus extension produces no observable predictions distinguishable from the reference model within the relevant sensitivity domain, it provides no independent empirical support for a new physical mechanism.

Failure Condition 4 — Observational Conflict

If the model produces predictions incompatible with established observations and cannot resolve the disagreement through a physically justified revision, the model is disfavored.

Failure Condition 5 — Unjustified Parameter Adjustment

If agreement with observations requires unconstrained parameter changes without independent physical justification, the model's explanatory value is weakened.

Failure Condition 6 — Failure of Independent Reproduction

If a computational or observational result cannot be independently reproduced using sufficiently documented methods and evidence, it cannot be treated as a robust scientific finding.

Failure Condition 7 — Unsupported Physical Interface

If a proposed physical boundary or interaction cannot be distinguished from an observational boundary, a mathematical convenience, or an ordinary cosmological effect, the physical-interface interpretation remains unsupported.

Failure Condition 8 — Instability or Causal Inconsistency

If the derived equations produce unacceptable instabilities, pathological propagation, or an ill-posed initial-value problem under the model's intended physical conditions, the model must be revised or rejected.

Failure Condition 9 — No Added Explanatory Value

If the proposed extension reproduces established model predictions without adding a justified physical explanation or a distinguishable empirical consequence, it must not be presented as an independently confirmed new cosmological mechanism.

These conditions do not prohibit theoretical exploration.

They define requirements for moving from conceptual speculation toward stronger scientific claims.

Failure of a particular model version does not automatically disprove every conceivable alternative. Any revision must, however, be explicitly documented, mathematically justified, and subjected to renewed testing. A model must not be repeatedly altered solely to avoid unfavorable evidence without recording the changes and their consequences.

11.14. Proposed Mathematical Development Program

The following sequence establishes a practical development path for the Magnetron Hypothesis.

No stage should be considered complete merely because a conceptual description has been written.

Stage 1 — Define the Cosmological Geometry

Select the spacetime metric and spatial topology to be investigated.

Specify:

- Coordinate conventions.
- Metric signature.
- Units.
- Curvature assumptions.
- Homogeneity and isotropy assumptions.
- The intended physical domain.

Stage 2 — Define the Additional Physical Degrees of Freedom

Identify whether the hypothesis requires:

- An additional scalar field.
- A vector field.
- A tensor field.
- An additional matter sector.
- A modified geometric structure.
- Another explicitly defined physical degree of freedom.

Specify the physical meaning and measurable properties of each proposed component.

Stage 3 — Construct the Governing Equations

Derive the field equations from a consistent action or specify a well-defined phenomenological model.

Document:

- The independent variables.
- The mathematical assumptions.
- The parameter definitions.
- The units and dimensions.
- The derivation steps.
- The domain of validity.

Check mathematical closure and dimensional consistency.

Stage 4 — Establish Conservation Relationships

Derive the associated stress-energy tensor and demonstrate consistency with the relevant conservation equations.

Specify any energy-momentum transfer terms and their physical origin.

Identify whether the proposed model is separately conserved or contains interactions between components.

Stage 5 — Define Initial and Boundary Conditions

Specify the physical domain, initial conditions, and any proposed interface conditions.

If multiple spacetime regions are involved, define their mathematical relationship.

Demonstrate that the resulting mathematical problem is sufficiently well-defined for analysis.

Stage 6 — Derive Cosmological Evolution

Determine how the proposed model changes:

- The scale factor.
- The expansion rate.
- Energy density.
- Pressure.
- Perturbation evolution.
- Relevant geometric quantities.

Investigate limiting behavior, stability, and perturbations.

Stage 7 — Generate Observable Predictions

Calculate predicted changes in measurable cosmological observables.

Identify the parameters controlling each prediction and quantify the expected uncertainty.

Distinguish effects that are unique to the proposed mechanism from effects already produced by standard cosmological components.

Stage 8 — Compare Against Established Models

Use suitable observational datasets, likelihoods, covariance information, and statistical methods.

Evaluate both improvements and conflicts across independent observational channels.

Document data selection, preprocessing, masks, calibration, parameter priors, and analysis assumptions.

Stage 9 — Conduct Independent Review

Make assumptions, equations, computational methods, and results available for technical examination where possible.

Preserve unsuccessful results, alternative interpretations, and revisions.

Independent reviewers must be able to determine which results are theoretical derivations, which are numerical outputs, and which are empirical observations.

11.15. Computational Implementation and Verification Requirements

The mathematical development program requires a corresponding computational framework.

A numerical implementation can assist with solving differential equations, exploring parameter space, evaluating stability, and generating predictions. It cannot establish the physical truth of an assumed model merely by producing a successful computation.

The computational program should be organized into distinct verification and validation activities.

11.15.1. Equation and Parameter Registry

Each implemented equation should be linked to:

- Its mathematical definition.
- Its source or derivation.
- Its units.
- Its assumptions.
- Its parameter definitions.
- Its applicable domain.
- Its verification tests.
- Its intended scientific use.

Each model version should receive a unique identifier.

Changes to equations or parameters should be recorded so that results can be traced to the exact model version that produced them.

11.15.2. Numerical Verification

Numerical verification should include:

- Dimensional consistency checks.
- Limiting-case tests.
- Analytic-solution comparisons where available.
- Convergence studies.
- Step-size sensitivity.
- Numerical stability checks.
- Independent implementation comparisons.
- Reproducibility checks.

A numerical solver should be tested against problems with known solutions before being used to generate new cosmological predictions.

11.15.3. Physical Validation

Physical validation asks whether the model adequately represents the physical system for its intended purpose.

It requires comparison with appropriate physical evidence, independent observations, and established theoretical constraints.

A software test passing does not establish that the physical model is correct.

Likewise, a successful numerical integration does not establish that the equations being integrated describe the universe.

11.15.4. Data Provenance and Reproducibility

Every computational result should preserve:

- Source data.
- Data version.
- Data acquisition or publication details.
- Preprocessing procedures.
- Model version.
- Parameter values.
- Numerical settings.
- Software dependencies.
- Random seeds, where relevant.
- Output files.
- Verification records.

The original data should be preserved separately from processed derivatives.

A reproducible analysis should allow another researcher to identify the exact inputs, code, assumptions, and numerical settings that produced the reported result.

11.15.5. Scope of Existing Nexus Verification

The Nexus laboratory contains historical mathematical and software verification records. Such records are relevant to the reliability of the implemented calculations within their defined test scope.

They must be described accurately.

Numerical verification of uncertainty propagation, covariance handling, finite-difference comparisons, or energy-balance software does not independently establish:

- The existence of an additional cosmological sector.
- A physical interface between cosmological domains.
- Global cosmic rotation.
- A magnetron-like cosmological mechanism.
- Energy extraction from the quantum vacuum.
- Agreement between a Nexus cosmological prediction and observational data.

Those claims require their own physical and observational evidence.

The computational program should preserve the distinction between software verification, mathematical verification, simulation, and empirical validation.

11.16. Research Status of the Magnetron Hypothesis

The present research status must be recorded explicitly.

Research element| Present status
Conceptual bounded-domain proposal| Defined conceptually
Observable versus physical boundary distinction| Established distinction in the research framework
Alternative spatial topology| Established mathematical research area
Additional cosmological sector| General mathematical formulation available
Homogeneous equation-of-state representation| Established mathematical framework
Energy-momentum exchange formalism| Established general framework
Nexus-specific physical field| Not yet defined
Nexus-specific interaction mechanism| Not yet derived
Physical interface with encompassing domain| Not yet established
Nexus-specific covariant action| Not yet derived
Nexus-specific cosmological solution| Not yet derived
Stability analysis of a Nexus-specific solution| Not yet completed
Distinctive observational prediction| Not yet calculated
Independent empirical validation of the Magnetron mechanism| Not yet achieved

This status record prevents preliminary mathematical architecture from being mistaken for a completed cosmological theory.

The distinction between established mathematical frameworks and Nexus-specific physical results must remain visible throughout the manuscript.

The Magnetron Hypothesis remains a research proposition whose scientific development depends on whether a physically coherent, mathematically closed, and observationally distinguishable model can be constructed.

11.17. Conclusion: The Standard of Mathematical Accountability

The Magnetron Hypothesis raises questions about bounded cosmological domains, possible additional physical sectors, energy-momentum exchange, and the relationship between local and encompassing systems.

These questions can be investigated scientifically only through precise mathematical definitions, physically justified assumptions, and testable predictions.

This section establishes three distinct development pathways:

1. Finite or multiply connected cosmological topology.
2. An additional cosmological physical sector.
3. A physically defined interaction with an encompassing domain.

These pathways are not equivalent, and none currently establishes the existence of a universal magnetron.

The mathematical foundations presented here provide a framework for investigating those possibilities without prematurely assigning physical meaning to undefined variables or unexplained interaction terms.

The next research stage is to select a mathematically precise model, derive its governing equations, assess its stability and conservation properties, and determine whether it produces observable consequences that cannot be explained equally well by established cosmology.

The governing principle is:

A cosmological hypothesis earns scientific standing through mathematical consistency, physical definition, observational compatibility, independent reproducibility, and predictions that survive attempts to falsify it.

The Nexus framework must remain open to the possibility that some of its original interpretations will require substantial revision or rejection.

That willingness to revise is not a weakness in the research program. It is a necessary condition of scientific progress.

11.18. Technical Notes and Qualifications

Note 1 — Metric and unit conventions.
The FLRW metric is written with signature ((-+++)). The Friedmann equations in Sections 11.2 and 11.5 use natural units with (c=1), and (\rho) and (p) are expressed as energy density and pressure in compatible units. Any numerical implementation using SI units must restore the relevant factors of (c) consistently.

Note 2 — Cosmological constant bookkeeping.
The cosmological constant is retained on the geometric side of the Einstein field equations in this chapter. If a later formulation transfers it to an effective stress-energy tensor, the convention must be changed consistently. It must not be counted in both places.

Note 3 — Equation-of-state scaling.
The relation (\rho_X\propto a^{-3(1+w_X)}) assumes a homogeneous component that is separately conserved and has constant (w_X). It does not apply unchanged to an interacting sector or a time-dependent equation of state.

Note 4 — Interaction convention.
The sign convention for (Q) is explicitly defined in Section 11.6. Positive (Q) transfers energy into the matter component and out of (X). Other conventions are possible, but every equation and interpretation must use one consistent convention.

Note 5 — Covariant versus global conservation.
The condition (\nabla_\mu T^{\mu\nu}=0) describes local covariant conservation of the total stress-energy tensor. It must not be interpreted automatically as a globally conserved total energy for an arbitrary expanding cosmological spacetime.

Note 6 — Junction conditions.
The Israel junction formalism applies to specified hypersurface matching constructions under its mathematical assumptions. Its availability does not establish the existence of a physical cosmological interface. A Nexus application requires explicit metrics, matching conditions, and surface stress-energy where appropriate.

Note 7 — Vorticity.
The vorticity tensor depends on the selected four-velocity congruence. Its interpretation is not independent of the chosen cosmological model. Vorticity, shear, and anisotropic expansion are distinct quantities.

Note 8 — Cosmic topology.
Constraints from matched-circle searches apply to the configurations and observational assumptions investigated. A null result in one search method does not exclude all possible spatial topologies.

Note 9 — Model-dependent cosmological parameters.
The Planck values quoted in Section 11.2 and Section 11.10 are inferred under specified cosmological assumptions and data combinations. They must not be presented as model-independent measurements.

Note 10 — Statistical comparison.
The covariance-weighted separation (D^2) is a diagnostic of prediction-vector separation under a specified covariance. It is not a substitute for likelihood analysis, Bayesian evidence, or a complete treatment of parameter refitting and systematic uncertainty.

Note 11 — BIC.
The BIC is an approximate large-sample criterion. Its application requires attention to the likelihood structure, effective sample-size assumptions, and parameter regularity. It should not be applied mechanically to a correlated data vector.

Note 12 — Numerical verification and physical validation.
A successful software or numerical verification establishes only the behavior of the tested implementation within the scope of its tests. It does not establish that the physical model is correct or that its proposed cosmological entities exist.

Note 13 — Status of the Magnetron mechanism.
The action and additional-sector equations in this chapter are general modeling templates. No Nexus-specific field action, derived interface, completed cosmological solution, distinctive observational prediction, or independent empirical confirmation is claimed.

Note 14 — Research revisions.
Any later change to the proposed mechanism, mathematical assumptions, model parameters, or observational predictions must be recorded as a new model version. An unsuccessful result must be preserved and must not be silently replaced by a revised result.
