Editorial and Scientific Qualification

This chapter provides a structured mathematical development framework for the Magnetron Hypothesis. It incorporates established results from general relativity, homogeneous cosmology, stress-energy conservation, spatial topology, relativistic junction theory, and statistical model comparison.

The additional-sector equations, interaction terms, action-based construction, and possible interface structures are general mathematical modeling frameworks. They have not yet been derived from a Nexus-specific physical mechanism.

The Magnetron Hypothesis does not currently possess a completed Nexus-specific field theory, derived physical interface, validated cosmological solution, distinctive observational prediction, or independent empirical confirmation.

The three development pathways must remain separately identified unless a later derivation establishes a mathematically consistent relationship among them.

The bibliography provides foundational and directly relevant sources. Final publication consolidation must still verify the complete source-to-claim correspondence, editions, page references where needed, and any later corrections or errata.

The research status must be updated only when supporting mathematical, computational, or observational evidence has been documented and independently assessed.



SECTION 12 — THE NEXUS COSMOLOGICAL SIMULATION AND OBSERVATIONAL VALIDATION PROGRAM

Computational Model Development, Numerical Experiments, Observational Comparison, and Independent Scientific Testing

Research Program: Nexus Sovereign
Human Research Author: Alpha One
AI-Assisted Research and Editorial Contributions: Axiom and other contributing AI systems, subject to documented contribution records
Document Version: 1.1 — Revised Research Draft
Classification: Interdisciplinary Scientific Research

---

12.1. Introduction: From Mathematical Formulation to Computational Investigation

Section 11 established the mathematical development requirements for the Magnetron Hypothesis. It identified several distinct pathways through which a bounded-domain cosmological proposal might be investigated, including spatial topology, additional cosmological physical sectors, and a possible interaction with a larger encompassing domain.

The next requirement is to determine how these mathematical proposals can be translated into computational models and compared with observational evidence.

This section establishes the Nexus Cosmological Simulation and Observational Validation Program (NCSOVP).

Its purpose is to create a controlled research procedure for:

1. Constructing mathematically specified cosmological models.
2. Establishing a reproducible implementation of a standard cosmological reference model.
3. Developing and testing additional theoretical components.
4. Simulating the predicted behavior of specified models.
5. Comparing predictions with appropriate observational datasets.
6. Evaluating statistical uncertainty and systematic effects.
7. Comparing competing physical explanations.
8. Identifying predictions that distinguish Nexus proposals from established alternatives.
9. Preserving computational provenance and reproducibility.
10. Supporting independent verification and possible falsification.

The program begins with a distinction that governs every subsequent result:

A simulation demonstrates the consequences of the equations, parameters, initial conditions, and assumptions supplied to it. It does not independently demonstrate that those equations and assumptions describe physical reality.

A computational result may be mathematically correct while the underlying physical model remains incomplete, unsupported, or incorrect.

Likewise, a model may reproduce an observational dataset because its parameters have been adjusted to that dataset, without possessing a uniquely justified physical mechanism.

The Nexus simulation program must therefore be designed to challenge its own assumptions rather than merely produce results consistent with its initial expectations.

12.1.1. Computational Verification and Physical Validation

Two distinct questions must be addressed.

Computational verification asks whether the numerical implementation correctly solves the specified mathematical problem.

It includes:

- Correct implementation of equations.
- Dimensional consistency.
- Numerical stability.
- Convergence.
- Reproducibility.
- Correct handling of numerical error.
- Agreement with analytical solutions or independent implementations where available.

Physical validation asks whether the mathematical model adequately represents the physical system for its intended purpose.

It requires:

- A defensible physical interpretation.
- Compatibility with established observations.
- Appropriate comparison with competing models.
- Independent evidence.
- Sensitivity to relevant physical assumptions.
- Predictions that can be tested.

Passing software tests demonstrates only that specified implementation requirements have been satisfied under the tested conditions.

It does not independently establish the physical accuracy of a cosmological hypothesis.

12.1.2. Four Levels of Computational Evidence

The research program will distinguish four levels of computational evidence.

Level 1 — Mathematical demonstration

A calculation demonstrates the consequences of a defined equation or mathematical construction.

Level 2 — Numerical verification

A computational implementation is shown to solve the specified mathematical problem within documented numerical tolerances.

Level 3 — Observational comparison

The model's predictions are compared with appropriate observational data using documented statistical methods.

Level 4 — Independent empirical assessment

Independent researchers or analysis teams examine the data, implementation, assumptions, and conclusions and determine whether the reported findings are reproducible and scientifically defensible.

These levels are cumulative only when their requirements are actually met. A Level 2 result does not automatically become Level 3 or Level 4 evidence.

The program must record the achieved level for every model and analysis.

---

12.2. The Simulation Hierarchy

The research program will employ four progressively more demanding model categories.

Each category has a different scientific status and computational requirement.

MODEL A — STANDARD COSMOLOGICAL REFERENCE MODEL

The first model is the spatially flat six-parameter (\Lambda)CDM cosmology, used as the principal reference framework.

It provides a well-developed baseline against which alternative models can be compared.

Its homogeneous background expansion is described by the Friedmann equations. Detailed predictions for the cosmic microwave background and large-scale structure require additional perturbation calculations, radiation and neutrino physics, recombination modeling, and numerical Boltzmann solvers where appropriate.

The reference model must be reproduced and independently checked before Nexus-specific modifications are introduced.

MODEL B — FINITE OR MULTIPLY CONNECTED SPATIAL TOPOLOGY

The second category investigates whether a finite spatial domain or nontrivial spatial topology produces observable consequences.

This category does not automatically require an external energy source or a physical boundary wall.

A universe can have finite spatial volume without possessing a material edge.

The simulation objective is to investigate the mathematical and observational consequences of a specified spatial manifold, including possible changes to spatial correlations, allowed perturbation modes, and patterns in cosmological observations.

MODEL C — ADDITIONAL COSMOLOGICAL PHYSICAL SECTOR

The third category introduces an explicitly defined additional component, denoted (X).

This component may provide a phenomenological starting point for exploring modified expansion histories or energy exchange.

It must have mathematically specified properties, including its energy density, pressure, equation of state, and any permitted interaction with established components.

A generic additional fluid is not, by itself, a Magnetron mechanism.

It becomes relevant to the Magnetron Hypothesis only if a physically motivated derivation connects its properties to the proposed mechanism.

MODEL D — HYPOTHETICAL EXTERNAL-DOMAIN INTERACTION

The fourth category concerns the more speculative possibility that the modeled cosmological domain interacts with a physically defined encompassing domain.

This category cannot be treated as an ordinary numerical modification until the proposed geometry, domain relationship, interface or coupling, interaction law, and conservation requirements have been established.

A simulation that simply inserts an arbitrary external energy term would demonstrate only the behavior of that chosen mathematical term.

It would not establish the existence of an external domain.

Model D therefore remains conditional on the development of a consistent physical theory.

12.2.1. Separation of Model Categories

These four categories must remain separately identified.

Results obtained in one category must not be presented as evidence for another unless a mathematical derivation establishes the connection.

In particular:

- A finite spatial topology does not establish an external physical domain.
- An additional cosmological fluid does not establish a magnetron-like mechanism.
- A modified expansion history does not establish an energy source outside the observable universe.
- A numerical interface model does not establish that a physical interface exists.
- A successful fit does not establish that the model's proposed physical interpretation is correct.

The simulation hierarchy exists to make these distinctions operational.

---

12.3. Establishing the Standard Cosmological Baseline

The first computational requirement is a reliable reference model.

For a homogeneous and isotropic universe, the expansion history can be represented through the scale factor (a(t)).

The Hubble parameter is:

[
H(t)=\frac{\dot a(t)}{a(t)}.
]

For a simplified spatially flat reference cosmology containing radiation, pressureless matter, and a cosmological constant:

[
H^2(a)

H_0^2
\left[
\Omega_{r,0}a^{-4}
+
\Omega_{m,0}a^{-3}
+
\Omega_{\Lambda,0}
\right].
]

Here:

- (H_0) is the present-day Hubble parameter.
- (\Omega_{r,0}) is the present radiation density parameter.
- (\Omega_{m,0}) is the present matter density parameter.
- (\Omega_{\Lambda,0}) is the present cosmological-constant density parameter.
- (a) is the scale factor normalized to unity today.

For this simplified flat model:

[
\Omega_{r,0}
+
\Omega_{m,0}
+
\Omega_{\Lambda,0}
=1.
]

The equation describes background expansion under the stated assumptions.

It is a useful starting point for initial numerical development, but it is not a complete precision-cosmology implementation.

12.3.1. Radiation, Matter, and Neutrino Treatment

A precision implementation must distinguish the physical components included in the reference model.

Radiation includes photons and relativistic species. Matter includes baryons and cold dark matter, with neutrino contributions depending on their masses and dynamical regime.

Massive neutrinos do not remain perfectly described by a single (a^{-4}) or (a^{-3}) scaling law throughout cosmic history. Their energy density evolves between relativistic and nonrelativistic behavior.

A more complete background expression is therefore:

[
H^2(a)

H_0^2
\left[
\Omega_{\gamma,0}a^{-4}
+
\Omega_{\nu}(a)
+
\Omega_{b,0}a^{-3}
+
\Omega_{c,0}a^{-3}
+
\Omega_{\Lambda,0}
\right],
]

for a spatially flat model with a cosmological constant, using a consistent definition of the neutrino contribution.

Here:

- (\Omega_{\gamma,0}) describes present photon density.
- (\Omega_{\nu}(a)) represents the scale-factor-dependent neutrino contribution under the selected neutrino model.
- (\Omega_{b,0}) describes present baryon density.
- (\Omega_{c,0}) describes present cold-dark-matter density.

A complete implementation must document the assumed neutrino masses, effective relativistic degrees of freedom, and treatment of the transition between relativistic and nonrelativistic behavior.

The simplified radiation-plus-matter equation remains useful for controlled analytical and numerical tests, provided its limited scope is stated.

12.3.2. Density Parameters and Curvature

For a general FLRW background, the density parameters and curvature contribution satisfy the appropriate Friedmann constraint.

Using the conventional curvature density parameter:

[
\Omega_{k,0}

-\frac{kc^2}{a_0^2H_0^2},
]

the background expansion can be written in a form containing:

[
\Omega_{k,0}a^{-2}.
]

The sign convention must remain consistent with the selected FLRW metric and curvature normalization.

The flat reference model sets:

[
\Omega_{k,0}=0.
]

A topology investigation must not automatically be represented by changing the curvature parameter. Global topology and local spatial curvature are distinct properties.

12.3.3. Redshift and Scale Factor

The cosmological scale factor is related to redshift by:

[
a=\frac{1}{1+z},
]

where (z) is cosmological redshift and the present scale factor is normalized to unity.

This relationship allows the expansion history to be evaluated across different cosmological epochs.

The Hubble function can therefore be written as:

[
H(z)=H(a)\bigg|_{a=(1+z)^{-1}}.
]

The redshift convention and reference normalization must be documented in every numerical implementation.

12.3.4. Numerical Integration

The background expansion can be integrated using:

[
\frac{da}{dt}=aH(a).
]

Alternatively, redshift can be used as the independent variable:

[
\frac{dt}{dz}

-\frac{1}{(1+z)H(z)}.
]

The negative sign reflects the fact that redshift decreases as cosmological time advances in an expanding universe.

An equivalent expression is:

[
\frac{dz}{dt}=-(1+z)H(z).
]

These equations provide a manageable starting point for numerical development.

They are appropriate for initial NTL cosmological work because they do not require a complete CMB Boltzmann solver.

However, the integration must specify:

- Initial scale factor or redshift.
- Initial time convention.
- Cosmological parameter values.
- Numerical solver.
- Absolute and relative tolerances.
- Integration interval.
- Treatment of singular or poorly conditioned regions.
- Convergence criteria.

A numerical result without these settings is not fully reproducible.

12.3.5. Cosmological Distance Measures

The background expansion can also be used to calculate cosmological distances.

The radial comoving distance is:

[
D_C(z)

c\int_0^z\frac{dz'}{H(z')}.
]

For a spatially flat universe:

[
D_M(z)=D_C(z).
]

For nonzero spatial curvature, the transverse comoving distance (D_M) is obtained from the appropriate curvature-dependent mapping of (D_C). The mapping must use the same curvature convention as the background metric.

The luminosity distance and angular-diameter distance are:

[
D_L(z)=(1+z)D_M(z),
]

[
D_A(z)=\frac{D_M(z)}{1+z}.
]

These relationships assume the standard metric propagation framework and the appropriate cosmological distance definitions.

They provide important outputs for comparison with supernova and baryon acoustic oscillation observations.

12.3.6. Baseline Acceptance

Before any Nexus-specific model is evaluated, the reference implementation must:

- Reproduce known analytical limits.
- Use documented cosmological parameters.
- Demonstrate numerical convergence.
- Preserve dimensional consistency.
- Compare against an independent implementation where practical.
- Produce results consistent with the selected reference model.
- Preserve the exact software version and parameter configuration.
- Record numerical tolerances and solver settings.
- Preserve the data and output files required for reconstruction.

The baseline must not be modified merely to make an alternative hypothesis appear more favorable.

Any departure from the reference model must be explicitly documented and justified.

---

12.4. Computational Development of Finite Spatial Topology

A finite or multiply connected universe represents a geometrical and topological possibility distinct from a physical cavity surrounded by a material wall.

Spatial geometry and topology must be defined independently.

A flat spatial geometry may be locally Euclidean while possessing a nontrivial global topology.

A simple illustrative construction is a three-dimensional toroidal space formed by identifying opposite faces of a spatial cell.

The local geometry can remain flat even though the global spatial manifold has finite volume.

This example illustrates why finite volume and physical enclosure must not be treated as equivalent.

12.4.1. Mathematical Definition of the Spatial Manifold

A topology simulation must specify the spatial manifold being investigated.

It should identify:

- The local spatial geometry.
- The global topology.
- The allowed coordinate identifications.
- The characteristic topological scales.
- The observer's position within the manifold.
- The relevant spatial eigenmodes.
- The assumed primordial perturbation statistics.

A finite computational box with periodic numerical boundary conditions is not automatically a physically justified model of cosmic topology.

Periodic boundary conditions may be useful numerical approximations, but their physical interpretation must be established separately.

12.4.2. Computational Questions

A topology investigation should examine:

1. Whether the proposed spatial topology is mathematically consistent.
2. Whether the topology introduces a characteristic length scale.
3. Whether that scale is compatible with observational constraints.
4. Whether repeated spatial patterns or correlations are predicted.
5. Whether the allowed perturbation modes differ from those of the reference model.
6. Whether predicted signatures survive realistic observational limitations.
7. Whether the result depends strongly on the observer's position.

12.4.3. Observable Signatures

Possible observational consequences include:

- Correlations between apparently separated regions of the CMB.
- Matched-circle patterns under suitable geometrical conditions.
- Changes in large-scale angular correlation functions.
- Modifications to allowed spatial perturbation modes.
- Changes in the covariance structure of cosmological observables.
- Other statistical signatures associated with the specified topology.

The mathematical calculation must connect the spatial manifold to the observable predictions.

A simulation that merely displays repeated patterns is insufficient. The model must calculate how the topology affects the relevant perturbation modes and observable quantities.

12.4.4. Observational Limitations

The absence of a detected signature does not automatically exclude every possible finite topology.

It does, however, constrain the models and parameter ranges capable of producing detectable effects.

The analysis must account for:

- The observer's location.
- The scale of the proposed topology.
- The finite resolution of the observations.
- Instrumental noise.
- Foreground contamination.
- Sky masks.
- Statistical uncertainty.
- The assumed primordial perturbation model.

12.4.5. Interpretation Boundary

A successful topology simulation would establish that a particular geometrical model produces specified mathematical or observational consequences.

It would not establish that the universe is a physical cavity, that a material wall exists, or that an external energy-generating mechanism is present.

Those are separate hypotheses requiring separate mathematical development and evidence.

---

12.5. Introducing an Additional Cosmological Sector

The additional-sector model provides a controlled mathematical framework for investigating whether a new cosmological component could alter the expansion history.

Let the additional component have energy density:

[
\rho_X
]

and pressure:

[
p_X.
]

A simple equation-of-state parameterization is:

[
p_X=w_X\rho_X,
]

where (w_X) is dimensionless.

For a noninteracting component with constant (w_X), the continuity equation is:

[
\dot\rho_X+3H(\rho_X+p_X)=0.
]

Substitution of the equation of state gives:

[
\dot\rho_X+3H(1+w_X)\rho_X=0.
]

The resulting solution is:

[
\rho_X(a)

\rho_{X,0}a^{-3(1+w_X)}.
]

This expression is a standard phenomenological fluid relationship.

It does not identify the physical origin of the component.

12.5.1. Modified Background Expansion

For a spatially flat model containing radiation, matter, a cosmological constant, and an additional component:

[
H^2(a)

\frac{8\pi G}{3}
\left[
\rho_r(a)
+
\rho_m(a)
+
\rho_X(a)
\right]
+
\frac{\Lambda c^2}{3},
]

where the densities are expressed consistently as mass-equivalent densities in this SI form.

Alternatively, with energy densities and natural units, the corresponding expression is written using the matching natural-unit convention.

This equation assumes that the additional component is consistently incorporated into the gravitational field equations.

If (X) is intended to replace or reinterpret the cosmological constant rather than supplement it, the model must avoid counting the same physical contribution twice.

12.5.2. Time-Dependent Equation of State

A more general phenomenological model may allow:

[
w_X=w_X(a).
]

For a separately conserved homogeneous component, the density evolves according to:

[
\rho_X(a)

\rho_{X,0}
\exp
\left[
-3\int_1^a
\frac{1+w_X(a')}{a'},da'
\right].
]

The constant-(w_X) expression is recovered when (w_X(a)) is constant.

A time-dependent equation of state introduces additional modeling freedom. It must therefore be constrained by physically defensible assumptions or derived from a specified underlying theory.

An arbitrary function chosen to improve a fit is not a physical mechanism.

12.5.3. Energy Exchange

If the additional sector exchanges energy with matter, a phenomenological interaction can be introduced.

Using natural units and a specified sign convention:

[
\dot\rho_m+3H(\rho_m+p_m)=Q,
]

[
\dot\rho_X+3H(\rho_X+p_X)=-Q.
]

Here (Q) describes the rate of energy exchange per unit physical volume in the homogeneous background model.

The opposite signs ensure that the exchange terms cancel when the component equations are summed.

The interaction function (Q) must be specified mathematically.

Its functional form cannot be selected solely because it produces a preferred result.

A physically meaningful model must also address the covariant interaction, momentum transfer, perturbations, stability, and causal consistency.

12.5.4. Status of the Additional-Sector Model

The additional-sector framework is a possible mathematical research route.

It is not evidence that a Magnetron exists.

A Nexus-specific physical interpretation requires a derivation of the component's properties and interaction law from an independently defensible theoretical construction.

---

12.6. External-Domain Interaction: Requirements Before Simulation

The most speculative part of the Magnetron Hypothesis proposes that the modeled cosmological domain may be embedded within or interact with a larger encompassing physical domain.

This proposal requires more than a modified expansion equation.

At minimum, a viable model must define:

- The encompassing spacetime or physical domain.
- The mathematical relationship between the domains.
- The interface or coupling structure, if one exists.
- The degrees of freedom participating in the interaction.
- The interaction equations.
- The relevant conservation laws.
- The initial and boundary conditions.
- The physical observables affected by the interaction.

If the proposal uses an interface between spacetime regions, the relevant gravitational matching conditions must be specified.

If it uses a field propagating through a larger domain, the field equations and coupling terms must be derived.

If it uses an effective description, the assumptions under which that description is valid must be documented.

12.6.1. A Conditional Simulation Gate

Model D should not proceed to observational fitting until the theory has passed the following minimum requirements:

1. A defined mathematical domain.
2. A consistent set of governing equations.
3. A defined interaction mechanism.
4. Conservation-law compatibility.
5. A stable or otherwise physically interpretable solution.
6. A defined relationship between model parameters and observables.
7. A sufficiently specified initial-value or boundary-value problem.
8. A documented treatment of perturbations and relevant stability conditions.
9. A demonstrated numerical method appropriate to the equations.
10. A defined set of failure conditions.

Without these elements, a numerical demonstration would be a visualization of assumptions rather than a test of a physical theory.

12.6.2. No Assumed External Energy Supply

An unexplained term in a numerical energy equation must not be interpreted as an external energy source.

The origin, physical meaning, and conservation accounting of every term must be identified.

The possibility of an external domain remains a research question, not an established cosmological result.

12.6.3. Model D Development Status

Until the mathematical closure requirements are satisfied, Model D should remain a theoretical development track rather than a parameter-fitting exercise.

Exploratory numerical demonstrations may be used to study mathematical behavior, but their outputs must be labeled as conditional illustrations.

They must not be presented as simulations of an established physical external-domain mechanism.

---

12.7. Observational Data and Comparison Strategy

A cosmological model must be tested against observations rather than judged by visual similarity or mathematical elegance alone.

The Nexus program should prioritize multiple observational categories.

Each dataset must have a documented release, selection procedure, measurement definition, covariance treatment, and analysis configuration.

12.7.1. Cosmic Microwave Background

The CMB provides measurements of temperature and polarization anisotropies across angular scales.

A model that seeks to modify standard cosmology must determine whether its predicted angular power spectra are compatible with the observed data.

Relevant spectra include:

[
C_\ell^{TT},\qquad
C_\ell^{TE},\qquad
C_\ell^{EE},
]

and, where relevant:

[
C_\ell^{BB}.
]

Here:

- (T) denotes temperature anisotropy.
- (E) denotes E-mode polarization.
- (B) denotes B-mode polarization.
- (\ell) denotes angular multipole.

Additional observables may include CMB lensing and higher-order statistical quantities.

The theoretical spectra are predictions of a specified cosmological model. Observationally estimated spectra are affected by finite sky coverage, instrumental noise, masks, foregrounds, beam effects, and data-processing choices.

The two must not be treated as identical objects.

A valid comparison requires the appropriate observational likelihood or a justified approximation to it.

12.7.2. CMB Polarization and Map-Level Analysis

Temperature and polarization contain different information.

Polarization analysis requires the treatment of Stokes parameters and their transformation into E- and B-mode components.

A CMB analysis must document:

- Frequency channels.
- Map products.
- Mask definitions.
- Foreground treatment.
- Beam and calibration information.
- Noise characterization.
- Spectrum estimation method.
- Likelihood implementation.
- Covariance assumptions.

A comparison using only a visually appealing temperature map is insufficient.

For nontrivial topology or statistical anisotropy, a standard isotropic power-spectrum likelihood may not capture all relevant information. The likelihood must be appropriate to the model being tested.

12.7.3. Baryon Acoustic Oscillations

Baryon acoustic oscillations provide a standard ruler used to constrain cosmological distances and the expansion history.

The analysis may involve measurements of:

[
\frac{D_M(z)}{r_d}
]

and:

[
\frac{D_H(z)}{r_d},
]

where:

[
D_H(z)=\frac{c}{H(z)}.
]

Here:

- (D_M(z)) is the transverse comoving distance.
- (D_H(z)) is the Hubble distance.
- (r_d) is the sound horizon at the baryon drag epoch.

The exact observables and covariance must follow the selected survey's published data products.

Some surveys also report other distance combinations, such as:

[
\frac{D_V(z)}{r_d}.
]

The analysis must use the definitions appropriate to the selected dataset.

The sound horizon is itself model-dependent because it depends on the pre-drag expansion and baryon-photon physics. Its treatment must be consistent between the model prediction and the observational likelihood.

12.7.4. Type Ia Supernovae

Type Ia supernova observations constrain the relationship between redshift and luminosity distance.

For a model predicting luminosity distance (D_L(z)), the theoretical distance modulus is:

[
\mu(z)

5\log_{10}
\left(
\frac{D_L(z)}{10,\mathrm{pc}}
\right).
]

The comparison must include:

- Photometric calibration.
- Light-curve standardization.
- Selection effects.
- Intrinsic scatter.
- Covariance.
- Absolute-magnitude treatment.
- Relevant nuisance parameters.

The absolute magnitude and cosmological distance scale can be degenerate in supernova-only analyses.

The statistical treatment must therefore reflect the actual dataset and any external calibration information used.

12.7.5. Large-Scale Structure and Gravitational Lensing

Additional tests may use:

- Galaxy clustering.
- Redshift-space distortions.
- Weak gravitational lensing.
- Matter power spectra.
- Structure-growth measurements.
- Cluster abundance, where suitable mass calibration is available.

These observations help test whether a proposed modification is compatible with both the background expansion and the growth of cosmological perturbations.

A model that reproduces the background expansion but predicts incompatible structure growth is not thereby validated.

12.7.6. Combined Evidence

A model should not be considered successful because it fits one observational category while producing serious contradictions elsewhere.

Where datasets are statistically compatible and their covariance is appropriately handled, combined analyses can provide stronger constraints.

The program must document whether datasets are:

- Statistically independent.
- Partially overlapping.
- Derived from common observations.
- Correlated through shared calibration or nuisance parameters.

Dataset combinations must not double-count measurements or assume statistical independence without justification.

---

12.8. Statistical Model Comparison

The simulation program requires a consistent method for comparing model predictions with observations.

Let the observed data vector be:

[
\mathbf d,
]

and let the model prediction be:

[
\mathbf m(\boldsymbol\theta),
]

where (\boldsymbol\theta) represents the model parameters.

For a Gaussian likelihood with a fixed covariance matrix (C), the chi-squared statistic is:

[
\chi^2

\left[
\mathbf d-\mathbf m(\boldsymbol\theta)
\right]^T
C^{-1}
\left[
\mathbf d-\mathbf m(\boldsymbol\theta)
\right].
]

The covariance matrix represents statistical relationships between measured data points.

It must not be replaced by a diagonal approximation unless that approximation is justified for the selected analysis.

12.8.1. Parameter Estimation

The research program should estimate parameters using documented priors and an appropriate sampling or optimization method.

The analysis should report:

- Parameter definitions.
- Prior distributions.
- Posterior distributions.
- Parameter correlations.
- Convergence diagnostics.
- Effective sample sizes where applicable.
- Sensitivity to prior choices.
- Relevant nuisance parameters.
- The treatment of parameter degeneracies.
- The likelihood implementation and version.

If Markov chain Monte Carlo is used, convergence must be evaluated using appropriate diagnostics rather than relying solely on a visually stable trace plot.

The number of generated samples is not, by itself, evidence of convergence.

12.8.2. Likelihood Completeness

For a Gaussian likelihood with a fixed covariance matrix, the quadratic chi-squared term is sufficient for comparisons in which the omitted normalization is common to the models.

If the covariance matrix depends on model parameters, the Gaussian log-likelihood must also include the determinant term:

[
-2\ln\mathcal L

\chi^2+\ln|C|+N\ln(2\pi),
]

where (N) is the dimension of the data vector under the stated Gaussian formulation.

The determinant term must not be discarded when it varies between models.

If the likelihood is non-Gaussian, a Gaussian approximation must be justified and its limitations documented.

12.8.3. Model Complexity

An alternative model may improve its fit simply by introducing more adjustable parameters.

Consequently, improved fit alone is insufficient to establish that the alternative is preferable.

Potential comparison methods include:

- Bayesian evidence.
- Akaike information criterion.
- Bayesian information criterion.
- Posterior predictive checks.
- Cross-validation where appropriate.
- Sensitivity analysis.
- Robustness tests under alternative data treatments.
- Parameter-identifiability analysis.

Bayesian evidence is:

[
Z_M

\int
\mathcal L(\mathbf d\mid\boldsymbol\theta,M)
\pi(\boldsymbol\theta\mid M)
,d\boldsymbol\theta,
]

where:

- (\mathcal L) is the likelihood.
- (\pi) is the normalized prior distribution.
- (M) identifies the model.

A comparison between two models may be expressed through the Bayes factor:

[
B_{10}=\frac{Z_{M_1}}{Z_{M_0}}.
]

The prior assumptions must be specified and justified because Bayesian evidence can depend strongly on prior volume.

The Bayesian information criterion is:

[
\mathrm{BIC}

k\ln(n)-2\ln(\mathcal L_{\max}),
]

where:

- (k) is the number of fitted parameters.
- (n) is the sample-size quantity appropriate to the approximation's assumptions.
- (\mathcal L_{\max}) is the maximum likelihood.

The BIC is an approximate model-comparison criterion. It is not interchangeable with Bayesian evidence and is not a universal solution for correlated cosmological datasets.

The choice of model-comparison method must be justified and documented before conclusions are drawn.

12.8.4. Residual Analysis

Residuals should be examined for:

- Systematic patterns.
- Scale dependence.
- Correlated deviations.
- Sensitivity to data selection.
- Possible calibration effects.
- Inadequate model assumptions.
- Outliers and data-quality problems.

A small overall chi-squared value does not guarantee that a model is physically correct.

A large value may indicate model inadequacy, underestimated uncertainties, unaccounted correlations, or data-processing problems.

Residual analysis should include appropriate diagnostic plots, standardized residuals where applicable, and posterior predictive checks.

12.8.5. Multiple Testing and Exploratory Analysis

Exploratory searches across many observables, parameter ranges, or data selections can produce apparently unusual results by chance.

The analysis must distinguish confirmatory tests from exploratory investigations.

Where many hypotheses or parameter combinations are examined, the report must describe the multiplicity of tests and the method used to interpret the resulting significance.

A result selected after extensive searching must not be presented as though it were a single pre-specified test.

---

12.9. Numerical Validation and Computational Reliability

A cosmological simulation is only as reliable as its numerical implementation.

The NCSOVP must therefore establish numerical validation requirements before interpreting model results.

12.9.1. Analytical Test Cases

Where analytical solutions exist, the numerical solver should reproduce them within defined tolerances.

Examples include:

- Simple matter-dominated expansion.
- Radiation-dominated expansion.
- Constant-equation-of-state fluid evolution.
- Limiting cases in which an additional component becomes negligible.
- Flat and curved background limits where analytical solutions are available.
- Other analytically tractable solutions appropriate to the equations implemented.

The expected solution and acceptance tolerance should be specified before the test is run.

12.9.2. Convergence Testing

A numerical solution should be tested using progressively refined time steps, integration tolerances, spatial resolutions, or other relevant numerical controls.

If the result changes substantially under reasonable refinement, the calculation has not demonstrated numerical convergence.

The acceptance tolerance must be specified before the result is evaluated.

Convergence should be assessed for the quantities actually used in the scientific analysis, not only for an internal solver variable.

12.9.3. Independent Implementation

Where practical, important results should be checked using an independent solver or a recognized reference implementation.

Agreement between two implementations can reduce the likelihood of an unnoticed coding error.

It does not establish the physical truth of the model, especially if both implementations use the same incorrect assumptions or equations.

Independent implementations should be compared using identical conventions, parameter values, initial conditions, and output definitions.

12.9.4. Numerical Error Separation

Reports must distinguish:

- Measurement uncertainty.
- Statistical uncertainty.
- Systematic uncertainty.
- Parameter uncertainty.
- Numerical approximation error.
- Model uncertainty.

These categories are related but not interchangeable.

A narrow numerical integration error does not imply a narrow physical uncertainty.

12.9.5. Solver and Software Verification

The computational record should identify:

- Solver algorithm.
- Integration method.
- Absolute and relative tolerances.
- Floating-point precision.
- Software version.
- Dependencies.
- Compiler or runtime details where relevant.
- Hardware or platform information when it materially affects reproducibility.
- Known numerical limitations.

The use of an established software package does not remove the need to verify its configuration and intended applicability.

12.9.6. Reproducibility Record

Every simulation should preserve:

- Model identifier.
- Equation set and model version.
- Parameter values and priors.
- Initial conditions.
- Numerical solver and configuration.
- Software versions and dependencies.
- Random seeds, where applicable.
- Dataset identifiers and releases.
- Data-processing configuration.
- Convergence results.
- Output files.
- Integrity hashes.
- Analysis logs.

This record is necessary for independent reconstruction of the analysis.

A hash can help detect changes to a file relative to a trusted reference. It does not establish that the underlying data or scientific interpretation is accurate.

---

12.10. Falsifiability and Pre-Registered Testing

The Nexus research program must identify in advance what results would count against each hypothesis.

A model that can accommodate every possible observation through unrestricted parameter changes is not meaningfully predictive.

12.10.1. Model Definition Before Fitting

Before examining final validation results, researchers should specify:

- The governing equations.
- The parameter space.
- The expected observables.
- The statistical method.
- The exclusion criteria.
- The principal alternative explanations.
- The conditions that would count as failure.
- The primary analysis.
- Relevant sensitivity analyses.
- The planned treatment of missing or unusable data.

This reduces the risk of changing the hypothesis after seeing the data.

Exploratory work remains legitimate, but it must be labeled as exploratory and distinguished from confirmatory testing.

12.10.2. Calibration and Validation

Where appropriate, data should be separated into calibration and validation roles.

However, cosmological datasets often contain correlated measurements.

A simple random split of correlated observations may produce misleadingly optimistic validation results.

The split must therefore respect the statistical structure of the dataset.

Independent surveys, independent observational channels, or carefully designed held-out analyses may provide more defensible validation strategies.

12.10.3. Null Tests

The program should include tests designed to reveal whether an apparent signal could arise from:

- Instrumental artifacts.
- Foreground contamination.
- Data-processing choices.
- Calibration errors.
- Statistical fluctuations.
- Selection effects.
- Model flexibility.
- Incorrect uncertainty assumptions.
- Unrecognized correlations.

A null result must be reported.

It must not be discarded merely because it fails to support the proposed hypothesis.

12.10.4. Independent Replication

A result should be considered substantially strengthened only when independent researchers can reproduce the analysis using documented methods and appropriately independent checks.

Replication should include the ability to obtain a null or contradictory result when the underlying evidence does not support the original claim.

Differences in software, instrumentation, data processing, and analysis methods should be documented rather than concealed.

---

12.11. Nexus-Specific Prediction Requirements

The Magnetron Hypothesis must eventually produce predictions that distinguish it from existing cosmological models.

A proposal that merely reproduces the predictions of (\Lambda)CDM without adding a testable distinction does not establish an independent explanatory advantage.

Potential prediction categories include:

1. A mathematically specified deviation in the expansion history.
2. A distinctive spatial-topology signature.
3. A predicted modification to CMB correlation functions or power spectra.
4. A measurable effect from a defined additional cosmological sector.
5. A specific, independently testable consequence of a physically derived external-domain interaction.

Each proposed prediction must identify:

- The relevant equation.
- The parameter controlling the effect.
- The expected magnitude or allowed range.
- The observational channel.
- The uncertainty requirements.
- The competing conventional explanation.
- The observation that would contradict the prediction.

Parameter degeneracies must be considered.

A proposed signal that can be reproduced by ordinary changes to existing cosmological parameters may not distinguish the Nexus model.

12.11.1. Prediction and Sensitivity

The model should calculate whether its predicted difference is large enough to be measurable by the selected observational dataset.

A theoretically nonzero difference may remain empirically inaccessible if it is smaller than the relevant statistical or systematic uncertainty.

The research program should therefore identify:

- The expected signal magnitude.
- The measurement precision required.
- The relevant systematic-error floor.
- The parameter combinations controlling the effect.
- The observational dataset capable of testing it.
- The model assumptions that could suppress or mimic the signal.

12.11.2. Distinctive Prediction Register

Each Nexus-specific prediction should be entered into a prediction register containing:

Field| Required record
Prediction ID| Unique identifier
Model version| Exact equations and implementation
Observable| Defined measurable quantity
Reference model| Model against which it is compared
Predicted effect| Direction and magnitude or allowed range
Parameters| Variables controlling the effect
Uncertainty| Statistical, systematic, and theoretical
Dataset| Intended observational source
Failure condition| Result that contradicts the prediction
Status| Proposed, calculated, tested, supported, rejected, or inconclusive

A prediction must not be marked supported solely because a simulation produces the expected output. Observational evidence and appropriate comparison are required.

Until these requirements are met, the Magnetron Hypothesis remains a conceptual research program rather than a quantitatively validated cosmological model.

---

12.12. Implementation Architecture Within the Nexus Thermodynamic Laboratory

The Nexus Thermodynamic Laboratory provides the initial computational environment for developing the simulation program.

Its current implementation is a Python project in a Termux environment.

The existing software includes components for energy accounting, measurement modeling, calibration-related calculations, uncertainty analysis, and automated testing.

The present project does not yet implement a complete cosmological simulation or observational-validation pipeline.

Its existing demonstration dataset is not a cosmological observational dataset.

The proposed expansion should therefore proceed incrementally and should preserve a clear distinction between implemented capabilities and planned development.

STAGE A — BACKGROUND EXPANSION SOLVER

Develop a Python module capable of:

- Defining cosmological parameters.
- Evaluating (H(a)) or (H(z)).
- Numerically integrating the background expansion.
- Calculating selected cosmological distance measures.
- Comparing known limiting cases.
- Producing traceable output files.
- Recording numerical tolerances and solver settings.

This is the most practical first cosmological computation.

STAGE B — ADDITIONAL-FLUID MODEL

Extend the solver to support a phenomenological component (X).

The module should allow controlled variation of:

- Present-day density.
- Equation-of-state parameter.
- Time-dependent equation of state, if specified.
- Interaction terms, only when explicitly defined.
- Initial conditions.
- Numerical tolerances.

The model must retain a standard (\Lambda)CDM baseline for direct comparison.

STAGE C — PARAMETER AND SENSITIVITY ANALYSIS

Introduce controlled parameter sweeps and sensitivity calculations.

The objective is to identify which parameters materially affect the predicted expansion history and distance measures.

The program should record parameter ranges and distinguish physically motivated values from exploratory assumptions.

Sensitivity analysis should include parameter degeneracies where relevant. A parameter that appears influential in isolation may become poorly identifiable when other parameters are allowed to vary.

STAGE D — OBSERVATIONAL DISTANCE COMPARISON

Calculate model-dependent distance measures and compare them with appropriately selected public observational data.

The first comparisons should be limited to datasets with clearly documented definitions, uncertainties, and covariance.

The analysis should begin with a small number of well-understood observables before attempting combined multi-probe inference.

STAGE E — ADVANCED COSMOLOGICAL SOFTWARE INTEGRATION

When the theoretical model and computational requirements justify it, the research program may use established cosmological tools.

Examples include:

- CLASS, for cosmological background and perturbation calculations and associated Boltzmann-code functionality.
- CAMB, for CMB, lensing, matter power-spectrum, transfer-function, and background calculations.
- Cobaya, for Bayesian parameter inference and statistical sampling using supported theory and likelihood interfaces.
- HEALPix, for analysis and representation of data on the sphere.

These are established research tools, not Nexus-developed discoveries.

Their use requires appropriate installation, configuration, scientific understanding, and validation.

They must not be assumed to be available in the current Termux environment or treated as simple extensions of the existing energy-balance software.

The selected version, configuration, dependencies, and applicable license must be recorded.

STAGE F — FULL OBSERVATIONAL ANALYSIS

A mature analysis may require:

- CMB temperature and polarization likelihoods.
- Foreground and instrumental nuisance parameters.
- BAO measurements and covariance.
- Supernova distance measurements.
- Structure-growth observations.
- Model-comparison procedures.
- Independent numerical verification.
- Sufficient computing resources for the selected analysis.

This stage may require additional computational resources and a suitable desktop or research-computing environment.

The existing Android-based laboratory remains useful as a development and testing environment, but it should not be represented as a complete precision-cosmology platform.

12.12.1. Proposed Software Separation

The cosmological program should be implemented as a separate, clearly identified software layer within the wider NTL project.

A proposed architecture is:

nexus_thermo_lab/
│
├── src/
│   ├── energy_balance.py
│   ├── uncertainty.py
│   ├── calibration.py
│   ├── measurement_models.py
│   └── cosmology/
│       ├── parameters.py
│       ├── background.py
│       ├── distances.py
│       ├── fluids.py
│       ├── interactions.py
│       ├── topology.py
│       ├── likelihoods.py
│       └── model_registry.py
│
├── data/
│   ├── raw/
│   ├── processed/
│   └── reference/
│
├── configs/
│   ├── lcdm_baseline/
│   ├── topology_models/
│   └── additional_sector/
│
├── tests/
│   └── cosmology/
│
├── reports/
│   ├── cosmology/
│   └── verification/
│
└── scripts/
    ├── run_background.py
    ├── compare_distances.py
    └── validate_cosmology.py

This is a proposed structure, not a statement that these files or modules have already been implemented.

Existing laboratory modules should be preserved. The cosmological software should be added without silently replacing or altering historical research components.

12.12.2. Configuration and Model Versioning

Each model should be defined through a versioned configuration.

The configuration should record:

- Model identifier.
- Model version.
- Cosmological parameter values.
- Unit conventions.
- Initial conditions.
- Solver settings.
- Optional physical components.
- Interaction assumptions.
- Data references.
- Software dependencies.

The exact configuration used to generate a result must be preserved with that result.

Changing a parameter or equation should produce a traceable new configuration rather than silently modifying an earlier result.

---

12.13. Simulation Acceptance Criteria

A model should be considered ready for observational evaluation only after satisfying defined computational and theoretical requirements.

Requirement| Acceptance condition
Mathematical specification| Equations, variables, and assumptions are documented
Physical interpretation| Every model term has a defined physical or explicitly phenomenological role
Dimensional consistency| Equations and parameters use compatible units
Conservation consistency| Applicable conservation requirements are satisfied
Mathematical well-posedness| The problem is sufficiently defined for the intended calculation
Numerical stability| Results pass appropriate stability and convergence tests
Baseline comparison| The selected standard reference model is reproduced
Data compatibility| Observational definitions and covariance are respected
Statistical method| Likelihoods, priors, and comparison criteria are documented
Uncertainty accounting| Relevant statistical, systematic, parameter, and numerical uncertainties are addressed
Reproducibility| Inputs, code, dependencies, and configuration are preserved
Falsifiability| Conditions that contradict the model are stated
Independent review| Results can be examined and reproduced by others

Failure to meet a criterion must be documented rather than concealed by an adjusted parameter choice.

The acceptance status should be assigned to a specific model version and declared scope.

Passing a background-expansion test, for example, does not automatically establish that the same model has passed CMB, perturbation, or structure-formation validation.

12.13.1. Model-Specific Acceptance

Acceptance must also be specific to the model category.

Model A: Must reproduce the selected reference background and, when claimed, the relevant perturbation and observational calculations.

Model B: Must define a spatial manifold and calculate its relevant mathematical or observational consequences.

Model C: Must define the additional component, its conservation or interaction structure, and its effect on the relevant cosmological observables.

Model D: Must satisfy the additional physical and mathematical closure requirements described in Sections 11 and 12.6 before it can be treated as a physical simulation model.

No model category inherits acceptance merely because another category has passed its tests.

---

12.14. Interpretation of Possible Outcomes

The simulation program must allow for several scientifically legitimate outcomes.

OUTCOME A — NO DETECTABLE DIFFERENCE

The Nexus model may produce predictions indistinguishable from the standard reference model within available observational precision.

This would not establish an independent physical explanation.

It may indicate that the model is observationally degenerate with an existing theory.

OUTCOME B — IMPROVED FIT WITHOUT ROBUST MODEL PREFERENCE

An additional model may improve the fit to selected data but fail to justify its additional complexity.

This would motivate further examination rather than a claim of discovery.

OUTCOME C — OBSERVATIONAL INCOMPATIBILITY

The model may produce predictions that conflict with established observations.

Such a result would count against the tested formulation.

The model would require revision or rejection, depending on the nature and severity of the contradiction.

OUTCOME D — DISTINCTIVE, REPRODUCIBLE PREDICTION

A model may produce a prediction not shared by the reference framework and subsequently receive independent observational support.

This would justify further investigation.

It would still require scrutiny of alternative explanations, systematic effects, parameter degeneracies, and independent replication.

OUTCOME E — MATHEMATICAL OR COMPUTATIONAL INCONSISTENCY

The proposed equations may prove internally inconsistent, mathematically incomplete, numerically unstable, or incompatible with the required conservation relationships.

This would require correction or rejection of the affected model formulation before observational claims could be considered.

OUTCOME F — INCONCLUSIVE EVIDENCE

The available observations may be insufficient to distinguish the competing models.

In this case, the correct conclusion is that the evidence is inconclusive.

The absence of a decisive result must not be presented as confirmation.

All outcomes must be preserved in the research archive, including null results, failed calculations, unfavorable comparisons, and revised interpretations.

---

12.15. Research Implementation Milestones

The following milestones provide an ordered development plan for the NCSOVP.

MILESTONE 1 — REFERENCE COSMOLOGY

Implement and verify the background expansion equations for the selected standard reference model.

Deliverables:

- Documented parameter configuration.
- Reproducible background solver.
- Analytical limiting-case tests.
- Numerical convergence report.
- Comparison with an independent reference calculation where practical.

MILESTONE 2 — CONTROLLED ALTERNATIVE MODELS

Implement finite-topology investigations and phenomenological additional-sector models as separate research tracks.

Deliverables:

- Defined model assumptions.
- Separate configuration files.
- Explicit parameter definitions.
- Comparison with the unchanged reference model.

MILESTONE 3 — DISTANCE AND EXPANSION COMPARISON

Calculate expansion and distance observables for the reference and alternative models.

Deliverables:

- Predicted (H(z)).
- Relevant distance measures.
- Documented numerical uncertainties.
- Initial comparison with suitable observational datasets.

MILESTONE 4 — PERTURBATION AND CMB DEVELOPMENT

Where justified by the mathematical model, extend the analysis to perturbation evolution and CMB observables.

Deliverables:

- Perturbation equations.
- Stability analysis.
- Validated computational configuration.
- Appropriate likelihood and covariance treatment.

MILESTONE 5 — STATISTICAL MODEL COMPARISON

Evaluate whether an alternative model provides a defensible improvement over the reference framework.

Deliverables:

- Parameter-estimation records.
- Model-comparison results.
- Sensitivity and robustness analyses.
- Documented limitations and competing explanations.

MILESTONE 6 — NEXUS-SPECIFIC PREDICTION

Develop a mathematically defined Nexus prediction that can be distinguished from the reference framework.

Deliverables:

- Physical or phenomenological derivation.
- Defined observable.
- Predicted magnitude or range.
- Uncertainty assessment.
- Explicit failure condition.

MILESTONE 7 — INDEPENDENT SCIENTIFIC EXAMINATION

Make the relevant mathematical definitions, source code, datasets, configuration, and results available for independent examination.

Deliverables:

- Reproducibility package.
- Independent review record.
- Documented disagreements and limitations.
- Corrections or revised model versions where necessary.

These milestones are conditional on successful completion of the preceding requirements.

The computational development of topology models and additional-sector models may proceed as separate tracks.

Neither track validates an external-domain interaction without a separate physical derivation.

The development of a Nexus-specific external-domain model must follow the mathematical closure requirements established in Section 11.

Progress must be measured by validated results, not by the number of software features or simulations completed.

---

12.16. Research Governance, Data Integrity, and Scientific Reporting

A computational cosmology program requires more than equations and numerical solvers.

It also requires a reliable record of how every result was produced.

The NCSOVP should adopt the following research-governance requirements.

12.16.1. Preservation of Original Data

Original observational datasets must be preserved separately from processed or transformed data.

The research archive should identify:

- Original source.
- Data release.
- Download or acquisition date.
- Data version.
- File format.
- Units.
- Metadata.
- Processing history.
- Applicable access conditions.
- Integrity hashes.

Processed data must be traceable to the original source and the processing procedure used.

12.16.2. Data Provenance

Every reported result should be traceable through:

[
\text{Source Data}
\rightarrow
\text{Processing}
\rightarrow
\text{Model}
\rightarrow
\text{Numerical Analysis}
\rightarrow
\text{Statistical Evaluation}
\rightarrow
\text{Reported Result}.
]

Each transition must be documented.

A cryptographic hash can help establish that a file matches a previously recorded reference. It does not prove that the data are accurate, that the original measurement was valid, or that the scientific interpretation is correct.

12.16.3. Negative Results and Failed Analyses

Unsuccessful calculations, null results, rejected models, and unfavorable comparisons must be retained.

A result should not be removed from the research record merely because it does not support the initial hypothesis.

Corrections must be documented through versioned records.

12.16.4. Scientific Reporting

Every published computational result should identify:

- The exact model version.
- The equations used.
- The data release.
- The numerical implementation.
- The statistical method.
- The uncertainty treatment.
- The validation status.
- The limitations.
- The alternative explanations considered.

The report must distinguish completed work from planned work.

A proposed simulation pipeline must not be described as though it has already produced observational results.

---

12.17. Conclusion: Computational Evidence and Scientific Accountability

The Nexus Cosmological Simulation and Observational Validation Program establishes a structured pathway for moving from mathematical proposals toward computational investigation and empirical comparison.

It separates four model categories:

1. Standard cosmological reference modeling.
2. Finite or multiply connected spatial topology.
3. An additional cosmological physical sector.
4. A hypothetical interaction with an encompassing physical domain.

Each category has different mathematical requirements and different evidentiary limits.

The program begins with the reproduction of an established cosmological reference model. It then introduces controlled alternatives, tests numerical reliability, evaluates observational compatibility, and requires predictions that could distinguish the proposed framework from existing explanations.

The current Nexus Thermodynamic Laboratory provides an initial software foundation for measurement and energy-accounting development.

It does not yet constitute a completed cosmological simulation platform, a validated Magnetron model, or an observationally confirmed cosmological theory.

That distinction must remain explicit throughout the development process.

The scientific standard is:

A cosmological hypothesis earns credibility not by reproducing the expectations of its authors, but by surviving quantitative comparison with evidence that could have contradicted it.

The Nexus framework must remain open to null results, unfavorable comparisons, mathematical revision, and rejection of individual model formulations.

Computational sophistication is not a substitute for physical evidence. Mathematical consistency is necessary but not sufficient. Observational agreement must be assessed against competing explanations and independent evidence.

The purpose of the NCSOVP is therefore not to guarantee confirmation of the Magnetron Hypothesis.

Its purpose is to determine:

- Whether the hypothesis can be expressed mathematically.
- Whether its consequences can be calculated reliably.
- Whether those consequences are compatible with observations.
- Whether its predictions can distinguish it from existing explanations.
- Whether independent researchers can reproduce and critically examine the results.

The program is successful as a scientific process even if particular Nexus model formulations are ultimately rejected.

The objective is not to protect a preferred conclusion.

The objective is to determine what the evidence permits us to conclude.

---

12.18. Technical Notes and Qualifications

Note 1 — Scope of the simplified Friedmann equation.
The radiation-plus-matter-plus-cosmological-constant expression in Section 12.3 is a simplified background model. Precision calculations must document the treatment of photons, neutrinos, baryons, cold dark matter, curvature, and any additional physical components.

Note 2 — Neutrino evolution.
Massive neutrinos may transition between relativistic and nonrelativistic behavior. A precision implementation must use a treatment appropriate to their masses and distribution functions rather than assuming a single radiation or matter scaling law across all epochs.

Note 3 — Density and unit conventions.
The background equations must use a consistent convention for mass-equivalent density, energy density, pressure, and powers of the speed of light. Natural-unit and SI formulations must not be mixed without restoring the appropriate conversion factors.

Note 4 — Spatial topology.
Finite spatial volume does not imply a material boundary. Numerical periodic boundary conditions do not, by themselves, establish a physical cosmic topology. A topology model requires a specified spatial manifold and a calculation of its consequences.

Note 5 — Additional-sector bookkeeping.
The cosmological constant must not be counted both on the geometric side of the Einstein equations and as an additional stress-energy contribution. Any alternative convention must be defined consistently.

Note 6 — Energy exchange.
The background transfer function (Q) represents a homogeneous energy-transfer term under the stated sign convention. A complete interacting cosmological model must also specify the covariant transfer vector, momentum transfer, and perturbation equations.

Note 7 — CMB spectra and likelihoods.
The theoretical angular power spectra are not identical to observationally estimated band powers. A valid comparison must use the relevant likelihood, masks, beams, foreground models, noise treatment, and covariance information.

Note 8 — Topology and statistical anisotropy.
Some nontrivial topologies can produce signatures that are not fully captured by the usual statistically isotropic angular power spectra. The analysis method must be appropriate to the proposed topology.

Note 9 — BAO sound horizon.
The sound horizon (r_d) depends on early-universe physics. The model's prediction and the observational likelihood must use compatible definitions and assumptions.

Note 10 — Supernova absolute magnitude.
The supernova distance-modulus relation includes an absolute-magnitude calibration or equivalent nuisance parameter. Its treatment affects the inference of the absolute distance scale and must be documented.

Note 11 — Covariance-dependent likelihoods.
When a Gaussian covariance matrix depends on model parameters, its determinant contributes to the likelihood. Omitting this term can produce an incorrect model comparison.

Note 12 — Bayesian evidence.
Bayesian evidence depends on the model priors as well as the likelihood. Prior choices must be justified and sensitivity to them should be assessed.

Note 13 — BIC limitations.
The Bayesian information criterion is an approximation with assumptions about the likelihood, sample size, and parameter behavior. It should not be applied mechanically to correlated cosmological data vectors.

Note 14 — Data correlations.
CMB, BAO, supernova, lensing, and structure-growth datasets may share calibration, observations, or nuisance parameters. Combined analyses must account for overlap and covariance rather than assuming independence automatically.

Note 15 — Numerical convergence.
A solver's internal success flag is not a complete convergence demonstration. Relevant scientific outputs must be tested against changes in numerical resolution and solver tolerances.

Note 16 — Software independence.
Agreement between two codes is useful but does not eliminate shared theoretical or data-model errors. Independent verification should examine both implementation and assumptions.

Note 17 — Model uncertainty.
Parameter uncertainty and numerical uncertainty do not capture every uncertainty associated with an incomplete or misspecified physical model. Model uncertainty must be acknowledged separately.

Note 18 — Existing NTL capability.
The present laboratory contains software for selected measurement, energy-accounting, calibration, and uncertainty tasks. The cosmological solver, topology pipeline, Boltzmann calculations, and observational likelihood program described here are proposed development stages unless separately documented as implemented and tested.

Note 19 — External-domain interpretation.
A numerical term representing an additional energy contribution does not establish the existence of an external domain. Model D requires an independently justified mathematical and physical construction.

Note 20 — Research status.
No Nexus-specific cosmological simulation result, CMB likelihood analysis, BAO fit, supernova fit, or independent observational validation is claimed by this section.

---
